Lipschitz Bounds and Nonautonomous Integrals
Cristiana De Filippis & Giuseppe Mingione

Abstract
We provide a general approach to Lipschitz regularity of solutions for a large class of vector-valued, nonautonomous variational problems exhibiting nonuniform ellipticity. The functionals considered here range from those with unbalanced polynomial growth conditions to those with fast, exponential type growth. The results obtained are sharp with respect to all the data considered and yield new, optimal regularity criteria also in the classical uniformly elliptic case. We give a classification of different types of nonuniform ellipticity, accordingly identifying suitable conditions to get regularity theorems.



1
Introduction

The aim of this paper is to provide a comprehensive treatment of Lipschitz regularity of solutions for a very large class of vector-valued nonautonomous variational problems, involving integral functionals of the type

W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})\ni w\mapsto\mathcal{F}(w;\Omega):=\int_{\Omega}\left[F(x,Dw)-f\cdot w\right]\,dx\,.

(1.1)

Here
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open subset with
n\geq 2
and
N\geq 1
. In the following we shall assume the structure condition
F(x,Dw)\equiv\tilde{F}(x,|Dw|)
, which is natural in the vectorial case, where
\tilde{F}:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a suitably regular function (see Section
4.1
below for the precise assumptions).
The vector field
f\colon\Omega\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{N}
will be at least
L^{n}
-integrable

\displaystyle f\in L^{n}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})\,.

(1.2)

The notion of local minimizer used in this paper is quite standard in the literature.

Definition 1

A map
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
is a local minimizer of the functional
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1
) with
f\in L^{n}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
if, for every open subset
\tilde{\Omega}\Subset\Omega
, we have
\mathcal{F}(u;\tilde{\Omega})<\infty
and
\mathcal{F}(u;\tilde{\Omega})\leq\mathcal{F}(w;\tilde{\Omega})
holds for every competitor
w\in u+W^{1,1}_{0}(\tilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

In the rest of the paper we shall abbreviate local minimizer simply by minimizer. We just remark that, thanks to (
1.2
) and Sobolev embedding, requiring
\mathcal{F}(u;\tilde{\Omega})<\infty
for every
\tilde{\Omega}
in Definition
1
is the same to requiring
F(\cdot,Du)\in L^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
. In this paper we deal with the following, classical

Problem.

Find minimal regularity assumptions on
f
and
x\mapsto F(x,\cdot)
, guaranteeing local Lipschitz continuity of minima of the functional
\mathcal{F}
in
(
1.1
)
, provided this type of regularity holds when
f\equiv 0
and no
x
-dependence occurs, i.e.,
F(x,z)\equiv F(z)
.

Eventually, Lipschitz continuity opens the way to higher order regularity. In the problem above, a situation where no dependence on
x
typically occurs when considering frozen integrands of the type

z\mapsto F(x_{0},z)
, for some
x_{0}\in\Omega
. We recall that, under suitable growth conditions, the analysis of (
1.1
) usually involves the related Euler-Lagrange system

-\,{\rm div}(\tilde{a}(x,|Du|)Du)=f\,,\quad\ \tilde{a}(x,|Du|):=\frac{\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,|Du|)}{|Du|}\,.

(1.3)

Here
\tilde{F}^{\prime}
denotes the partial derivative of
\tilde{F}
with respect to the second variable.

Recently, Cianchi & Maz’ya
CM0
;
CM1
;
CM2
;
CM3
;
CM4
(global estimates) and Kuusi and the second author
KMstein
;
KMbull
;
KMjems
(local estimates), investigated the above problem in the uniformly elliptic, autonomous case, i.e.
\tilde{a}(x,|Du|)\equiv\tilde{a}(|Du|)
and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle-1<i_{\textnormal{a}}\leq\frac{\tilde{a}^{\prime}(t)t}{\tilde{a}(t)}\leq s_{\textnormal{a}}<\infty\quad\mbox{for every $t>0$}\\[10.0pt]
\mbox{$\tilde{a}\colon(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$ is of class $C^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(0,\infty)$}\,.\end{array}\right.

(1.4)

A special, yet important model, is given by the
p
-Laplacean system with coefficients

-\,{\rm div}(\mathfrak{c}(x)|Du|^{p-2}Du)=f\,,\quad p>1\,,\quad 0<\nu\leq\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)\leq L\,.

(1.5)

For this we have

Nonlinear Stein Theorem

KMstein
.
Let
u\in W^{1,p}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a weak solution to (
1.5
). If
f\in L(n,1)(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
, and
\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)
is Dini continuous, then
Du
is continuous.

In particular,
Du
is locally bounded. We recall that
f\in L(n,1)(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
means that

\|f\|_{L(n,1)(\Omega)}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}|\{x\in\Omega\,\colon|f(x)|>\lambda\}|^{1/n}\,d\lambda<\infty\,,

(1.6)

and also that
L^{q}\subset L(n,1)\subset L^{n}
for every
q>n
. Moreover, denoting by
\omega(\cdot)
the modulus of continuity of
\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)
, the Dini continuity of
\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)
amounts to require that

\int_{0}\omega(\varrho)\,\frac{d\varrho}{\varrho}<\infty\,.

(1.7)

The above theorem extends to general equations
KMbull
and to systems depending on forms
sil
; it also extends classical results of Uhlenbeck
Uh
and Uraltseva
Ur
; we again refer to Cianchi & Maz’ya
CM0
;
CM1
;
CM3
for global statements. The terminology is motivated by the fact that, for
\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)\equiv 1
and
p=2
, this is another classical result of Stein
St
. It is optimal both with respect to condition (
1.6
), as shown by Cianchi
CiGA
, and with respect to (
1.7
), as shown by Jin, Maz’ya & Van Schaftingen
mazyaex
. The relevant fact here is that the conditions on
f
and
\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)
implying local Lipschitz continuity are independent of
p
. In fact, when considering more general equations, they are independent of the vector field the divergence operator is applied to; for this, see
KMbull
, and
Baroni
for conditions (
1.3
)-(
1.4
).

In the case of nonuniformly elliptic operators, the problem of deriving sharp conditions with respect to data for Lipschitz regularity is considerably more difficult. This has been attacked only recently by Beck and the second author
BM
, but only for the case of autonomous functionals in the principal part, i.e.,
F(x,z)\equiv F(z)
(with some abuse of terminology, in this paper we shall refer to
F(x,z)\equiv F(z)
as to the autonomous case, no matter
f(\cdot)
can still be present in (
1.1
), to emphasize that coefficients
x
do not appear in the part of the integrand containing gradient terms). The outcome of
BM
is that, when
n>2
, condition (
1.6
) is still sufficient to guarantee the local Lipschitz regularity of minima, thereby revealing itself as a sort of universal property. In the case
n=2
, the alternative, slightly stonger borderline condition
L^{2}(\log L)^{\mathfrak{a}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
with
\mathfrak{a}>2
, implies Lipschitz continuity:

f\in L^{2}(\log L)^{\mathfrak{a}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})\Longleftrightarrow\int_{\Omega}|f|^{2}\log^{\mathfrak{a}}\left(\textnormal{e}+|f|\right)\,dx<\infty\,.

(1.8)

Therefore, in the nonuniformly, autonomous case, the condition on
f
can be summarized as

|f|\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}L(n,1)(\Omega)&\mbox{ if $n>2$}\\[4.0pt]
L^{2}(\log L)^{\mathfrak{a}}(\Omega)\,,\ \mathfrak{a}>2&\mbox{ if $n=2$}\,.\end{array}\right.

(1.9)

In this paper we deal with the general, fully nonautonomous case (
1.1
). This is by no means a technical extension as, in fact, when passing to the nonuniformly elliptic case, the role of coefficients drastically changes and they can no longer be treated via perturbation as in
KMstein
. To give a glimpse of the situation, let us consider the so called double phase functional

\begin{cases}\displaystyle w\mapsto\int_{\Omega}\left[H(x,Dw)-f\cdot w\right]\,dx\\
\displaystyle H(x,z):=\tilde{H}(x,|z|):=|z|^{p}/p+a(x)|z|^{q}/q\end{cases}

(1.10)

with
1<p<q
,
0\leq a(\cdot)\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. This functional has been originally introduced by Zhikov
Z1
;
Z2
in the setting of Homogeneization of strongly anisotropic materials, and the corresponding regularity theory has been studied at length starting by
BCM
;
CoM0
;
CoM
. The functional in (
1.10
) changes its rate of ellipticity/coercivity - from
p
to
q
- around the zero set
\{a(x)=0\}
. As shown in
sharp
;
FMM
, already when
f\equiv 0
, local minima fail to be continuous if the ratio
q/p
is too far from
1
, in dependence on the rate of Hölder continuity
\alpha
. Specifically, the condition

\frac{q}{p}\leq 1+\frac{\alpha}{n}\,,\qquad a(\cdot)\in C^{0,\alpha}(\Omega)\,,\qquad\alpha\in(0,1]

(1.11)

is necessary
sharp
;
FMM
and sufficient
BCM
to get gradient local continuity, thereby linking growth conditions of the integrand with respect to the gradient variable, to the smoothness of coefficients. In particular, classical Schauder’s theory generally fails. This is the main theme of this paper.
Condition in (
1.11
) reveals a typical phenomenon occurring when nonuniform ellipticity is directly generated by the presence of the
x
-variable as in (
1.10
). In this case, it is indeed the very presence of
x
making functionals as in (
1.10
) fail to meet the standard, two-sided polynomial conditions with the same exponent, i.e.,
H(x,z)\not\approx|z|^{p}
. We shall also deal with more drastic examples of such an interplay, as for instance

w\mapsto\int_{\Omega}\left[\mathfrak{c}_{1}(x)\exp(\mathfrak{c}_{2}(x)|Dw|^{p})-f\cdot w\right]\,dx\,,\qquad p>1\,,

(1.12)

where
0<\nu\leq\mathfrak{c}_{1}(\cdot),\mathfrak{c}_{2}(\cdot)\leq L
. Here the dependence on
x
becomes even more delicate as it makes the ellipticity rate vary more drastically.
Such integrands fail to satisfy the so-called
\triangle_{2}
-condition, i.e.,
\tilde{F}(x,2t)\not\lesssim\tilde{F}(x,t)
. This reflects in a loss of related integrability conditions on minimizers as one tries to use perturbation methods, that is, considering a specific point
x_{0}\in\Omega
and making small variations of
x
around
x_{0}
. In other words,
\exp(\mathfrak{c}_{2}(\cdot)|Dw|^{p})\in L^{1}
does not necessarily imply
\exp(\mathfrak{c}_{2}(x_{0})|Dw|^{p})\in L^{1}
, and vice versa, and plain perturbation methods are again banned. Exponential type functionals are classical in the Calculus of Variations starting by the work of Duc & Eells
DE
and Marcellini
M2
. In the nonautonomous version, they are treated for instance in the setting of weak KAM-theory by Evans
evans
, but under special assumptions and boundary conditions. More recent progress is in
DM
, for
f\equiv 0
.

Nonuniform ellipticity is a very classical topic in partial differential equations, and it is often motivated by geometric and physical problems. Seminal papers on this subject are for instance
DE
;
LU
;
Simon
;
Z1
;
Z2
; a classical monograph is Ivanov’s
ivanov
. In the setting of the Calculus of Variations there is a wide literature available, starting from the basic papers of Uraltseva & Urdaletova
UU
and Marcellini
M1
;
M2
;
M3
;
M4
. More recently, the study of the nonautonomous case has intensified; many papers have been devoted to study specific structures as well as genereal non-uniformly elliptic problems
BS
;
Bi
;
BM
;
BF1
;
BB2
;
BOR
;
BO
;
BY
;
Cellinastaicu
;
DeMi1
;
DeMi
;
DM
;
HO
;
HS
. Connections to related function spaces have been studied too
diening
;
HH
;
RR
.

The results obtained in this paper are very general and cover large classes of different models cases simultaneously. For this, a number of technical assumptions is necessary; see Section
4.5
below. Anyway, when applied to single models, such assumptions reveal to be minimal and produce sharp results. In the autonomous case
F(x,z)\equiv F(z)
, they coincide with the sharp ones introduced in
BM
. For this reason, and also to ease the reading, in this introductory part we shall present a few main corollaries of the general theorems, in connection to some relevant instances of nonuniformly elliptic functionals often considered in the literature. These models fall in three different general classes, detailed in Sections
1.1
-
1.3
below. We refer the reader to Section
2
for a full account of the notation used in this paper, while more remarks on nonuniform ellipticity are in Section
4.6
below.

1.1
Nonuniform ellipticity at polynomial rates

We start considering functionals with unbalanced polynomial growth conditions, of so-called
(p,q)
-type after Marcellini
M1
;
M2
. The idea is to provide general conditions on the partial map
x\mapsto F(x,\cdot)
implying regularity of minima and matching those suggested by counterexamples
sharp
;
FMM
. In this respect, we consider, for exponents
1<p\leq q
, the conditions

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\ F(x,z)=\tilde{F}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\quad\mbox{for all}\ (x,z)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}\\[3.0pt]
\nu(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2})^{p/2}\leq F(x,z)\leq\Lambda(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2})^{q/2}+\Lambda(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2})^{p/2}\\[3.0pt]
(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2})|\partial_{zz}F(x,z)|\leq\Lambda(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2})^{q/2}+\Lambda(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2})^{p/2}\\[3.0pt]
\nu(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(p-2)/2}|\xi|^{2}\leq\partial_{zz}F(x,z)\xi\cdot\xi\,,\end{array}\right.

(1.13)

for every choice of
z,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
|z|\not=0
. Here
0<\nu\leq 1\leq\Lambda
and
\mu\in[0,1]
are fixed constants.
\tilde{F}
satisfies (
4.1
)
2,3
below. We moreover assume that

t\mapsto\frac{\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)}{(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(p-2)/2}t}\quad\mbox{is non-decreasing}

(1.14)

for every
x\in\Omega
; as in the rest of the paper, we denote
\tilde{F}^{\prime}\equiv\partial_{t}\tilde{F}
.
As for the crucial dependence on
x
, we assume that

\begin{cases}\ |\partial_{x}\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)|\leq h(x)\left[(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(q-2)/2}t+(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(p-2)/2}t\right]\\
\ h(\cdot)\in L^{d}(\Omega),\ d>n\end{cases}

(1.15)

holds for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and every
t>0
. Of course,
F,\tilde{F}^{\prime},
are also assumed to be continuous on
\Omega\times(0,\infty)
, while
\tilde{F}^{\prime\prime}
and
\partial_{x}\tilde{F}
are Carathéodory regular. Using Sobolev regularity on coefficients, is a natural approach, also considered elsewhere. See for instance the paper
KM
in the case of uniformly elliptic integrals. As for the nonuniformly elliptic setting, this approach has been used in
EMM
; see also Marcellini’s survey
M4
for a general overview. We also mention that, over the last years, Sobolev coefficients have been systematically considered as a replacement of usual Lipschitz ones to find optimal conditions in several other fields of analysis and PDE; see for instance
bcrippa
;
FGS
;
crippadelellis
.

Theorem 1.1

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1
), under assumptions
(
1.13
)
-
(
1.15
)
. Assume that (
1.9
) holds and

\frac{q}{p}<1+\min\left\{\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{d},\mathfrak{m}_{p}\right\}\quad\mbox{with}\ \ \mathfrak{m}_{p}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{4(p-1)}{\vartheta p(n-2)}&\mbox{if}&n\geq 3\\[6.0pt]
\ \frac{2(p-1)}{\vartheta p}&\mbox{if}&n=2\,,\end{array}\right.

(1.16)

where
\vartheta=1
if
p\geq 2
and
\vartheta=2
otherwise. Then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
. When either
p\geq 2
or
f\equiv 0
,
(
1.16
)
can be replaced by

\frac{q}{p}<1+\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{d}\,.

(1.17)

Theorem
1.1
actually follows from Theorem
4.1
in Section
4.5
below and, as all the other results presented in this Introduction, comes along with explicit local a priori estimates. In particular, for splitting structures as

w\mapsto\int_{\Omega}\left[\mathfrak{c}(x)F(Dw)-f\cdot w\right]\,dx\,,\qquad 0<\nu\leq\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)\leq L\,,

(1.18)

Theorem
1.1
becomes

Theorem 1.2

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of the functional in (
1.18
), under assumptions
(
1.13
)
-
(
1.14
)
with
F(\cdot)\equiv F(z)\equiv\tilde{F}(|z|)
, and
f
as in (
1.9
). Assume that
\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)\in W^{1,d}(\Omega)
with
d>n
and that
(
1.16
)
is satisfied. Then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
. When either
p\geq 2
or
f\equiv 0
,
(
1.16
)
can be replaced by
(
1.17
)
.

For double phase functionals in (
1.10
), condition (
1.15
) again amounts to assume that
a(\cdot)\in W^{1,d}(\Omega)
, and indeed we have

Theorem 1.3

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of the functional in

(
1.10
)
, such that
0\leq a(\cdot)\in W^{1,d}(\Omega)
,
d>n
, and that

(
1.9
)
holds together with

q/p\leq 1+1/n-1/d\,,\ \mbox{if $n\geq 2$}\ \ \mbox{and, when $n=2$, also $q/p<p$}\,.

(1.19)

Then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
.

Theorem
1.3
allows to clarify in which sense assumptions (
1.15
) and (
1.17
) & (
1.19
) are sharp. Indeed, Sobolev-Morrey embedding yields
a(\cdot)\in C^{0,\alpha}
with
\alpha=1-n/d
. This last identity makes conditions (
1.11
) and (
1.17
) coincide. In turn, (
1.11
) is sharp by the counterexamples in
sharp
;
FMM
. Therefore, (
1.15
) is the sharp differentiable version of (
1.11
), which is stronger than (
1.11
), but weaker than assuming that
a(\cdot)
is Lipschitz. Lipschitz continuity of coefficients is typically assumed in the literature in the nonautonomous case (see for instance
M2
;
dark
and related references).

1.2
Nonuniform ellipticity at fast rates and a different phenomenon

A prototype we have in mind here is given by (
1.12
). Looking at the case of polynomial growth in Section
1.1
, from (
1.15
) and (
1.16
) we see that the required integrability rate of coefficients
d
increases with the ratio
q/p
. A naive, but seemingly natural bet, would then assert that the exponential case needs more stringent conditions on the integrability exponent
d
. On the contrary, the situation reverses, and any
d>n
implies local Lipschitz continuity:

Theorem 1.4

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of the functional in

(
1.12
)
, such that
\mathfrak{c}_{1}(\cdot),\mathfrak{c}_{2}(\cdot)\in W^{1,d}(\Omega)
with
d>n
and
f
satisfies
(
1.9
)
. Then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
.

The same applies to more general functionals with faster growth, involving arbitrary compositions of exponentials, and therefore even faster growth conditions.
Specifically, we fix exponent functions
\{p_{k}(\cdot)\}
and coefficients
\{\mathfrak{c}_{k}(\cdot)\}
, all defined on the open subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, such that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}1<p_{\mathfrak{m}}\leq p_{0}(\cdot)\leq p_{M}\,,\quad 0<p_{m}\leq p_{k}(\cdot)\leq p_{M}\,,\ \mbox{for $k\geq 1$}\\
0<\nu_{\mathfrak{m}}\leq\mathfrak{c}_{k}(\cdot)\leq L\,,\quad p_{k}(\cdot),\mathfrak{c}_{k}(\cdot)\in W^{1,d}(\Omega)\,,\ \ d>n\,,\ \mbox{for $k\geq 0$}\,.\end{cases}

(1.20)

We then inductively define, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the functions
{\bf e}_{k}\colon\Omega\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\bf e}_{k+1}(x,t)&:=&\exp\left(\mathfrak{c}_{k+1}(x)\left[{\bf e}_{k}(x,t)\right]^{p_{k+1}(x)}\right)\\[6.0pt]
{\bf e}_{0}(x,t)&:=&\exp\left(\mathfrak{c}_{0}(x)t^{p_{0}(x)}\right)\,,\end{array}\right.

(1.21)

and consider the variational integrals

w\mapsto\int_{\Omega}\left[{\bf e}_{k}(x,|Dw|)-f\cdot w\right]\,dx\,.

(1.22)

Functionals as in (
1.22
) have been studied at length in the literature also because they provide the best case study to test how far one can go in relaxing the standard uniform ellipticity assumptions; see
BM
;
M2
and related references. The nonautonomous case is of special interest as the sensitivity to the
x
-dependence is magnified by taking multiple compositions of exponentials; see comments after display (
1.12
). We have the following result, which, as also Theorem
1.4
, is completely new already in the case
f\equiv 0
:

Theorem 1.5

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of the functional in

(
1.22
)
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, under assumptions
(
1.20
)
and such that
f
satisfies
(
1.9
)
. Then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
.

In other words, this fact brings functionals as in (
1.12
) closer to the realm of uniformly elliptic ones. The next step comes in fact in the subsequent section.

1.3
New results in the uniformly elliptic setting

New results follow in the classical uniformly elliptic setting too. This time the model is

w\mapsto\int_{\Omega}\left[A(x,|Dw|)-f\cdot w\right]\,dx\,,\quad A(x,t):=\mathfrak{c}(x)\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{a}(s)s\,ds\,,

(1.23)

for
t\geq 0
, with (
1.4
) being in force and such that
0<\nu\leq\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)\leq L
. Under such conditions, every solution to the system in (
1.3
) is a minimizer of the functional in (
1.23
) and the second identity in (
1.3
) is automatically satisfied.

Theorem 1.6

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of the functional in

(
1.23
)
, under assumptions
(
1.4
)
. If
|f|,|D\mathfrak{c}|\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
as defined in
(
1.9
)
, then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
. Moreover, there exists a positive radius
R_{*}\equiv R_{*}(n,N,i_{\textnormal{a}},s_{\textnormal{a}},\linebreak\mathfrak{c}(\cdot))\leq 1
such that if
\mathcal{B}\Subset\Omega
is a ball with
\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})\leq R_{*}
, then the following estimate holds for

c\equiv c(n,N,\nu,L,i_{\textnormal{a}},s_{\textnormal{a}})
:

\|A(\cdot,\lvert Du\rvert)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}/2)}\leq c\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{\mathcal{B}}A(\cdot,\lvert Du\rvert)\,dx+c\|f\|_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B})}^{\frac{i_{\textnormal{a}}+2}{i_{\textnormal{a}}+1}}+c\,.

(1.24)

In other words,
f
and
D\mathfrak{c}
are this time required to have the same degree of regularity. Theorem
1.6
applies to (
1.5
) by taking
A(x,t)\equiv\mathfrak{c}(x)t^{p}/p
and it is sufficient to require that
D\mathfrak{c}\in L(n,1)(\Omega)
for
n>2
. This is, already when
f\equiv 0
, a new regularity criterion on coefficients, which goes beyond the known and classical one in (
1.7
). Indeed,
D\mathfrak{c}\in L(n,1)
implies that
\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)
is continuous
St
, but not necessarily with a modulus of continuity
\omega(\cdot)
satisfying (
1.7
). Moreover, this criterion works for the general cases as in (
1.23
), to which methods from
KMstein
do not apply under the only considered structure assumption (
1.4
). When considered in the special case (
1.5
), it is
i_{\textnormal{a}}=p-2
and estimate (
1.24
) gives back the classical one valid for the
p
-Laplacean system in (
1.5
); see for instance
KMstein
;
KMjems
.

1.4
Calderón-Zygmund theory

In Theorems
1.1
-
1.6
, we can replace (
1.9
) by the weaker
f\in L^{n}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
, getting, as a corresponding outcome, that
Du\in L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
for every
p\geq 1
; see Theorem
4.3
below. This result is new in the nonuniformly elliptic case and is in perfect accordance with the Nonlinear Calderón-Zygmund theory known for the uniformly elliptic one
KMbull
. For instance, considering the system in (
1.5
),
D\mathfrak{c}\in L^{n}
implies that
\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)\in
VMO, the space of functions with vanishing mean oscillations
sarason
. At this point,
Du\in L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
, for every
p\geq 1
, follows from the standard theory (see
BOR
;
DoK
and references). In fact, we provide the first Calderón-Zygmund type estimates in problems with non-polynomial growth conditions. An example is the following result, which is completely new already in the autonomous case:

Theorem 1.7

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of the functional in

(
1.22
)
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, under assumptions
(
1.20
)
with
n>2
, and such that
f\in L^{n}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Then
{\bf e}_{k}(\cdot,|Dw|)\in L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
for every
p\geq 1
.

1.5
Obstacles

Applications follow to obstacle problems, leading to completely new and sharp results, already in the classical, uniformly elliptic case. For instance, we give the first results for fast growth functionals as in (
1.12
), and these are new already in the case of smooth obstacles. For this we consider the functional

\mathcal{F}_{0}(w;\Omega):=\int_{\Omega}F(x,Dw)\,dx\;

(1.25)

defined on
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
, where
F
is for instance one of the integrands considered in Theorems
1.1
-
1.6
; here we of course consider the scalar case
N=1
. Next we consider a measurable function
\psi\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}

and the convex set
\mathcal{K}_{\psi}(\Omega):=\{w\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)\colon w(x)\geq\psi(x)\ \mbox{for a.e.}\ x\in\Omega\}
. We then say that a function
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)\cap\mathcal{K}_{\psi}(\Omega)
is a constrained local minimizer of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
if, for every open subset
\tilde{\Omega}\Subset\Omega
, we have
\mathcal{F}_{0}(u;\tilde{\Omega})<\infty
and if
\mathcal{F}_{0}(u;\tilde{\Omega})\leq\mathcal{F}_{0}(w;\tilde{\Omega})
holds for every competitor
w\in u+W^{1,1}_{0}(\tilde{\Omega})
such that
w\in\mathcal{K}_{\psi}(\tilde{\Omega})
. We then have the following, far reaching extension of classical theorems from
choe
;
CL
;
fuobs
;
KS
;
liebe2
:

Theorem 1.8

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
be a constrained local minimizer of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
in
(
1.25
)
, where
F\colon\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is one of the integrands from Theorems
1.1
-
1.6
with
p\geq 2,i_{\textnormal{a}}\geq 0
(whenever such parameters are involved). If
\psi\in W^{2,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
and
\lvert D^{2}\psi\rvert\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
, then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

This last result is new already in the classical
p
-Laplacean case
F(x,z)\equiv|z|^{p}/p
, for which it offers a criterion which is alternative to those given in
choe
;
CL
– see also
KL
for double phase type functionals. In such papers Lipschitz estimates are obtained assuming that
D\psi
is locally Hölder continuous. Here we trade this last condition with

\lvert D^{2}\psi\rvert\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
, that in turn implies the mere continuity of
D\psi
. This is essentially the same phenomenon
seen in Theorem
1.6
, where the condition
\lvert D\mathfrak{c}\rvert\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
replaces the Dini-continuity of
\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)
. We note that in the constrained versions of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
we can allow for
p\geq 1
provided
\mu>0
; for this see Remark
11
below.

1.6
Remarks and organization of the paper

Some of the methods here also extend to general scalar functionals, i.e., when minima and competitors are real valued functions. In this case there is no need to assume the radial structure
F(x,Dw)\equiv\tilde{F}(x,|Dw|)
. On the other hand, additional conditions ensuring the absence of the so-called Lavrentiev phenomenon are needed to build suitable approximation arguments, see for instance
DeMi
;
sharp
. The radial structure is usually assumed in the vectorial case, otherwise singular minimizers might occur, even when data are smooth
dark
;
SY1
. Again in the scalar case, we mention the recent, very interesting paper
HO
, where gradient regularity results are obtained for minimizers of functionals as in (
1.25
). These results cover functionals with polynomial growth and special structure - the double phase functional is an instance - under Hölder continuity assumptions on coefficients. Anyway, they miss to cover all the classes of integrands described in Sections
1.1
-
1.2
.

The rest of the paper goes as follows. After Sections
2
and
3
, containing notations and preliminaries, respectively, in Section
4
we describe in detail the assumptions and the main results of the paper, that is Theorems
4.1
-
4.4
in Section
4.5
. These will imply, directly or with a few additional arguments, Theorems
1.1
-
1.8
above. We then proceed to Section
5
, that contains the necessary approximation tools for the proofs. One word here: this is a delicate point, as the approximations considered must carefully match the shape of the a priori estimates found later, on one side, and reflect the original structure assumptions on the other. The core of the paper is Section
6
, where we derive all the main a priori estimates. The proofs here involve a series of ingredients. First, we employ a delicate version of Moser’s iteration scheme developed in Proposition
1
. This is based on a peculiar choice of test maps suited to the structural assumptions considered. It goes via a finite step procedure taking advantage of suitable smallness conditions; by the way, this is sufficient to get the basic
L^{p}
-estimates of Theorem
4.3
. In turn, this is a preliminary ingredient used to make a nonlinear potential theoretic approach work; this last one is encoded in the abstract iteration Lemma
1
below. This approach works in the case
n>2
, but breaks down in two dimensions
n=2
, where more difficulties appear, as for instance already noted in
BM
;
CM0
;
CM1
;
CM2
;
CM3
. In this case we take another path, as devised in Section
6.7
below. We use a different interpolation type approach, eventually culminating in the use of Lemma
1
again. Sections
7
features the proofs of Theorems
4.1
and
4.3
, combining the approximation scheme of Section
5
and the estimates in Section
6
. Section
8
contains the results and the proofs for the uniformly elliptic case, i.e., the proof of Theorem
4.2
; additional a priori estimates are included here. In Section
10
we demonstrate the derivation of Theorems
1.1
-
1.7
. These are all direct corollaries of the main results, but Theorem
1.3
. This in fact requires some additional arguments to reach the equality borderline case in the first bound from (
1.19
), thereby reconnecting Theorem
1.3
to the known literature
BCM
;
CoM0
;
CoM
in the case
f\equiv 0
; the details can be found in Section
10.2
. Section
11
contains applications to obstacle problems and the proof of Theorem
1.8
. Finally, Section
12
features some auxiliary technical facts aimed at making certain computations in Sections
6
and
8
legal.

2
Basic notation

In the following,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
denotes an open domain, and
n\geq 2
and there is no loss of generality, in assuming that
\Omega
is also bounded, as all our results are local in nature.
We denote by
c
a general constant larger than
1
. Different occurrences from line to line will be still denoted by
c
. Special occurrences will be denoted by
c_{*},\tilde{c}
or likewise. Relevant dependencies on parameters will be as usual emphasized by putting the corresponding parameters in parentheses. We denote by
B_{r}(x_{0}):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|x-x_{0}|<r\}
the open ball with center
x_{0}
and radius
r>0
; we omit denoting the center when it is not necessary, i.e.,
B\equiv B_{r}\equiv B_{r}(x_{0})
; this especially happens when various balls in the same context will share the same center. Given a ball
B
, we denote by
\texttt{r}(B)
its radius; with
\gamma
being a positive number, we denote by
\gamma B
the ball concentric to
B
, with radius
\gamma\texttt{r}(B)
, and set
B/\gamma\equiv(1/\gamma)B
. In denoting several function spaces like
L^{p}(\Omega),W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, we shall denote the vector valued version by
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k}),W^{1,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
in the case the maps considered take values in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We shall often abbreviate
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\equiv L^{p}(\Omega),W^{1,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\equiv W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. We denote
\{e^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{N}
and
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
standard bases for
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, respectively; we shall always assume
n\geq 2
and
N\geq 1
. The general second-order tensor of size
(N,n)
as
\zeta=\zeta_{i}^{\alpha}e^{\alpha}\otimes e_{i}
is identified with an element of
\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
. The Frobenius product of second-order tensors
z
and
\xi
is defined as
z\cdot\xi=z_{i}^{\alpha}\xi_{i}^{\alpha}
so that
\xi\cdot\xi=|\xi|^{2}
, and this is the norm we use here for tensors, vectors, matrixes; needless to say, we are using Einstein’s convention on repeated indexes. For instance, if
v,u\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, then
v\cdot u=v_{i}u_{i}
. The gradient of a map
u=u^{\alpha}e^{\alpha}
is thus defined as

Du=\partial_{x_{i}}u^{\alpha}e^{\alpha}\otimes e_{i}\equiv D_{i}u^{\alpha}e^{\alpha}\otimes e_{i}\,,

and the divergence of a tensor
\zeta=\zeta_{i}^{\alpha}e^{\alpha}\otimes e_{i}
as
\,{\rm div}\,\zeta=\partial_{x_{i}}\zeta_{i}^{\alpha}e^{\alpha}
. When dealing with the integrands of the type
F\colon\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}\to[0,\infty)
, as the one considered in (
1.1
), second differential of
\partial_{zz}F(\cdot,z)
is interpreted as
\partial_{zz}F(\cdot,z)=\partial_{z_{j}^{\beta}z_{i}^{\alpha}}F(\cdot,z)(e^{\alpha}\otimes e_{i})\otimes(e^{\beta}\otimes e_{j})
, whenever
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
. For the rest of the paper we shall keep the following notation:

E(t):=\sqrt{t^{2}+\mu^{2}}\,,\qquad E_{\varepsilon}(t):=\sqrt{t^{2}+(\mu+\varepsilon)^{2}}

(2.1)

for
t>0
,
\mu\in[0,1]
,
\varepsilon>0
.
With
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
being a measurable subset with bounded positive measure
0<|\mathcal{B}|<\infty
, and with
g\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
k\geq 1
, being a measurable map, we denote

(g)_{\mathcal{B}}\equiv\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{\mathcal{B}}g(x)\,dx:=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}|}\int_{\mathcal{B}}g(x)\,dx\,.

Finally, in the following we denote

\displaystyle 2^{*}:=\begin{cases}\ \frac{2n}{n-2}\quad&\mbox{if}\ \ n>2\\
\ \mbox{any number larger than}\ \ 2\quad&\mbox{if}\ \ n=2\,.\end{cases}

(2.2)

The actual value of
2^{*}
when
n=2
will be clear from the context.

3
Potentials, functions spaces, iteration lemmas

With
g\in L^{2}(B_{r}(x_{0});\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
B_{r}(x_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
being any ball,
n\geq 2
, we consider the following nonlinear potential, that will play a crucial role in this paper:

{\bf P}_{1}^{g}(x_{0},r):=\int_{0}^{r}\left(\varrho^{2}\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{\varrho}(x_{0})}|g|^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}\frac{d\varrho}{\varrho}\,.

This quantity naturally relates to the standard, truncated Riesz potential in the sense that

\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|g(x)|}{|x-x_{0}|^{n-1}}\,dx\lesssim{\bf P}_{1}^{g}(x_{0},2r)\,.

As a matter of fact,
{\bf P}_{1}^{g}
can be used as an effective replacement of the original Riesz potential when dealing with nonlinear problems. Actually, its mapping properties coincide with those of the classical Riesz potentials on those function spaces that are in a sense smaller than
L^{2}
. We refer to
KMbull
;
MH
for more information on such nonlinear potentials and for recent results on Nonlinear Potential Theory in the setting of this paper. The space
\mathfrak{X}(\cdot)
in (
1.9
) plays a special role to ensure the local boundedness of
{\bf P}_{1}
. Indeed, given concentric balls
B_{R}\subset B_{R+r}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with
r>0
and
R+r\leq 2
, and
g\in L^{2}(B_{R+r};\mathbb{R}^{k})
, the following inequalities hold:

\begin{cases}\left\|{\bf P}_{1}^{g}(\cdot,r)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R})}\leq c(n)\|g\|_{L(n,1)(B_{R+r})}\quad n>2\\
\left\|{\bf P}_{1}^{g}(\cdot,r)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R})}\leq c(\mathfrak{a})\|g\|_{L^{2}(\log L)^{\mathfrak{a}}(B_{R+r})}\quad n=2\,,\end{cases}

(3.1)

where, in the last of the two inequalities, it is
c(\mathfrak{a})\to\infty
when
\mathfrak{a}\searrow 2
. For this we refer to
BM
;
KMbull
. Note that in the right-hand side of (
3.1
)
2
we find the Luxemburg norm of the Orlicz space
L^{2}(\log L)^{\mathfrak{a}}
in (
1.8
), which is defined as

\|g\|_{{L^{2}(\log L)^{\mathfrak{a}}(B_{R+r})}}:=\inf\left\{\lambda>0\,\colon\int_{B_{R+r}}\left(\frac{|g|^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\log^{\mathfrak{a}}\left(\textnormal{e}+\frac{|g|}{\lambda}\right)\,dx\leq 1\right\}.

The two following inequalities are well-known and hold for any
\tau>0
:

\begin{cases}\|g\|_{L(n,1)(B_{R+r})}\leq c(n,\tau)\|g\|_{L^{n+\tau}(B_{R+r})}\\
\|g\|_{{L^{2}(\log L)^{\mathfrak{a}}(B_{R+r})}}\leq c(\mathfrak{a},\tau)\|g\|_{L^{2+\tau}(B_{R+r})}\,.\end{cases}

(3.2)

Recalling the standard notation
(v-\kappa)_{+}:=\max\{v-\kappa,0\}
, we select the following result from
(
BM
, Lemma 3.1)
, that incorporates the basic elements of De Giorgi’s iteration, as used in Nonlinear Potential Theory since the basic work of Kilpeläinen & Malý
kilmal
:

Lemma 1

Let
B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
,
\delta\in\left(0,1/2\right)
and
v\in W^{1,2}(B_{r_{0}}(x_{0}))
be non-negative and
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(B_{2r_{0}}(x_{0}))
. Assume that there exist positive constants
\tilde{c},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}\geq 1
and a number
\kappa_{0}\geq 0
such that for all
\kappa\geq\kappa_{0}
and every ball
B_{r}(x_{0})\subset B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})
the inequality

\displaystyle\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{r/2}(x_{0})}\lvert D(v-\kappa)_{+}\rvert^{2}\,dx\leq\frac{\tilde{c}M_{1}^{2}}{r^{2}}\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{r}(x_{0})}(v-\kappa)_{+}^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\tilde{c}M_{2}^{2}\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\lvert f_{1}\rvert^{2}\,dx+\tilde{c}M_{3}^{2}\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\lvert f_{2}\rvert^{2}\,dx

(3.3)

holds. If
x_{0}
is a Lebesgue point for
v
, then

\displaystyle v(x_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{0}+cM_{1}^{1+\max\left\{\delta,\frac{n-2}{2}\right\}}\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})}(v-\kappa_{0})_{+}^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\ +cM_{1}^{\max\left\{\delta,\frac{n-2}{2}\right\}}\left[M_{2}\mathbf{P}_{1}^{f_{1}}(x_{0},2r_{0})+M_{3}\mathbf{P}_{1}^{f_{2}}(x_{0},2r_{0})\right]

(3.4)

holds too, with
c\equiv c(n,\tilde{c},\delta)
.

We conclude with another, more classical iteration lemma (see references of
BM
).

Lemma 2

Let
\mathcal{Z}\colon[\varrho,\xi]\to[0,\infty)
be a bounded function. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
a_{1},a_{2},\mathfrak{b}\geq 0
be numbers. If

\mathcal{Z}(\tau_{1})\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{Z}(\tau_{2})+\frac{a_{1}}{(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})^{\mathfrak{b}}}+a_{2}

holds whenever
\xi\leq\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}\leq\varrho
,
then

\mathcal{Z}(\varrho)\leq\frac{ca_{1}}{(\varrho-\xi)^{\mathfrak{b}}}+ca_{2}

holds too, with
c\equiv c(\varepsilon,\mathfrak{b})
.

4
Assumptions and general results

In this section we are going to describe a number of conditions on the integrand
F
in (
1.1
) implying our main results, that is Theorems
4.1
-
4.4
in Section
4.5
below; in turn, these will imply Theorems
1.1
-
1.8
from the Introduction.

4.1
Basic structural assumptions, and consequences

We assume that
F
has radial structure, i.e., there exists
\tilde{F}:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ F(x,z)=\tilde{F}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\ \mbox{for all $(x,z)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}$}\\
\ t\mapsto\tilde{F}(x,t)\in C^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}[0,\infty)\cap C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(0,\infty)\quad\mbox{for all $x\in\Omega$}\\
\ x\mapsto\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)\in W^{1,n}(\Omega)\ \ \mbox{for every $t>0$}\,.\end{cases}

(4.1)

We assume in general that
\tilde{F},\tilde{F}^{\prime}
are continuous on
\Omega\times(0,\infty)
, while
\tilde{F}^{\prime\prime},\partial_{x}\tilde{F}^{\prime}
are instead Carathéodory regular on the same set. Of course we also assume that
\sup_{\Omega\times K}\partial_{zz}{F}
is finite for every compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}\setminus\{0_{R^{N\times n}}\}
. Now we describe the minimal and standard assumptions qualifying the functional in (
1.1
) as elliptic. For this, we use three locally bounded functions
g_{i}\colon\Omega\times(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
, for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, in the sense that
\sup_{\Omega\times K}g_{i}
is finite, for every compact subset
K\subset(0,\infty)
. The first two
g_{1},g_{2}
are continuous and serve to bound the lowest and the largest eigenvalues of
\partial_{zz}F
, respectively. The third one
g_{3}
is Carathéodory regular and controls the growth of derivatives with respect to
x
. Specifically, we assume that there exists
T>0
such that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ z\mapsto F(x,z)\ \ &\mbox{is convex for all $x\in\Omega$}\\
\ \lvert\partial_{zz}F(x,z)\rvert\leq g_{2}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\ \ &\mbox{for all}\ x\in\Omega\ \mbox{on}\ \{\lvert z\rvert\geq T\}\\
\ g_{1}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}\leq\partial_{zz}F(x,z)\xi\cdot\xi\ \ &\mbox{on}\ \{\lvert z\rvert\geq T\}\ \mbox{and for all $x\in\Omega$}\\
\ |\partial_{x}\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)|\leq h(x)g_{3}(x,t)\ \ &\mbox{for a.e.\,$x\in\Omega$ and every $t>0$}\,,\end{cases}

(4.2)

where (
4.2
)
3
holds for every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
,
h(\cdot)\in L^{n}(\Omega)
and we assume also that

\inf_{x\in\Omega}g_{1}(x,T)>0\,.

(4.3)

Using as in (
1.3
) the notation

\tilde{a}(x,t):=\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)/t
, defined for
(x,t)\in\Omega\times(0,\infty)
,
we assume that for fixed numbers
\gamma>1
,
\mu\in[0,1]
, and for every
x\in\Omega
, the minimal
\gamma
-superlinear growth of the lowest eigenvalue of
\partial_{zz}F(x,\cdot)
, that is

t\mapsto\frac{\tilde{a}(x,t)}{(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}\ \mbox{and}\ t\mapsto\frac{g_{1}(x,t)}{(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}\ \ \mbox{are non-decreasing on}\ (0,\infty).

(4.4)

Note that the global convexity of
F(\cdot)
in (
4.2
)
1
implies, arguing as in (
4.9
)-(
4.11
) below, that
\tilde{a}(\cdot)\geq 0
, that is,
t\mapsto\tilde{F}(x,t)
is non-decreasing on
[0,\infty)
, for every
x\in\Omega
.

Remark 1

When considering partial derivatives of
F
and
g_{1}
in the
x
-variable, as well as the function
g_{3}
, properties will be given a.e. with respect to
x
in the Carath́eodory sense. For instance, (
4.2
)
4
means there exists a negligible set
N\subset\Omega
such that (
4.2
)
4
holds for every
t>0
, whenever
x\in\Omega\setminus N
. The same will apply later when considering for instance (
4.16
)-(
4.17
) and (
5.13
).

Remark 2

In most of the relevant models it turns out to be
\tilde{a}(\cdot)\equiv g_{1}(\cdot)
, and this justifies the double assumption in (
4.4
). Assumptions in (
4.2
) are bound to quantify ellipticity of
\partial_{zz}F(\cdot,z)
only outside the ball
\{|z|<T\}
(recall we are assuming
\nu>0
), and this allows to cover functionals loosing their ellipticity properties on a bounded set of
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
. This condition not only adds more generality, but helps simplifying the treatment in the case of nonuniformly elliptic problems. We could do the same also with respect to
\partial_{x}\tilde{F}^{\prime}
in (
4.2
)
4
, but we prefer not, as this would only add technical difficulties, while not covering more examples.

Let us derive a few consequences of (
4.1
)-(
4.4
). First, for every
x\in\Omega
it holds that

0<s\leq t\Longrightarrow\begin{cases}g_{1}(x,s)s\leq g_{1}(x,t)t\\
a(x,s)s\leq a(x,t)t\,.\end{cases}

(4.5)

Indeed, as
t\mapsto(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(\gamma-2)/2}t
is non-decreasing, we have

\displaystyle g_{1}(x,s)s

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{1}(x,s)}{(s^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}(s^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}s

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{00}}}{{\leq}}\frac{g_{1}(x,t)}{(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}t=g_{1}(x,t)t\,.

The same argument works for
t\mapsto a(\cdot,t)t
.
Moreover, again from (
4.4
), it readily follows that

\begin{cases}\nu(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\leq g_{1}(x,t)\ \mbox{for}\ t\geq T\\
0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{infima})}}{{<}}\nu:=\displaystyle\min\left\{\frac{\inf_{x\in\Omega}g_{1}(x,T)}{(T^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}},1\right\}\leq 1\,.\end{cases}

(4.6)

From the very definition of
\tilde{a}(\cdot)
, for
(x,t)\in\Omega\times(0,\infty)
we have

F(x,z)-F(x,0_{\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}})=\tilde{F}(x,|z|)-\tilde{F}(x,0)=\int_{0}^{|z|}\tilde{a}(x,s)s\,ds

(4.7)

and therefore
\partial_{xz}F(x,|z|)=\partial_{x}\tilde{a}(x,|z|)\otimes z=[\partial_{x}\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,|z|)/|z|]\otimes z
holds for
|z|\not=0
.
It follows that (
4.1
)
3
and (
4.2
)
4
imply that
x\mapsto\partial_{z}F(x,z)\in W^{1,n}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
such that
|z|\not=0
, and

|\partial_{xz}F(x,z)|,|\partial_{x}\tilde{a}(x,\lvert z\rvert)||z|\leq h(x)g_{3}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\ \mbox{on $\{|z|>0\}$, a.e. $x\in\Omega$}\,.

(4.8)

Again from the second-last display it follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{zz}F(x,z)=\partial_{z}[\tilde{a}(x,\lvert z\rvert)z]=\tilde{a}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\mathds{I}_{N\times n}+\tilde{a}^{\prime}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\lvert z\rvert\frac{z\otimes z}{\lvert z\rvert^{2}}

(4.9)

holds for
|z|\not=0
,
so that, using (
4.2
)
2,3
with
\xi=z
and
\xi\perp z
in (
4.9
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ \tilde{a}(x,\lvert z\rvert)+\tilde{a}^{\prime}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\lvert z\rvert\geq g_{1}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\\
\ \tilde{a}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\geq g_{1}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\end{cases}\ \mbox{for $(x,z)\in\Omega\times\left\{\lvert z\rvert\geq T\right\}$}

(4.10)

and

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ \tilde{a}(x,\lvert z\rvert)+\tilde{a}^{\prime}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\lvert z\rvert\leq g_{2}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\\
\ \tilde{a}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\leq g_{2}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\end{cases}\ \mbox{for $(x,z)\in\Omega\times\left\{\lvert z\rvert\geq T\right\}$}\,.

(4.11)

In particular, it follows that

\begin{cases}\ 0<g_{1}(\cdot,t)\leq g_{2}(\cdot,t)&\mbox{for $t\geq T$}\\
\ \tilde{a}(\cdot,t)\leq c(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(\gamma-2)/2}&\mbox{for $0<t\leq T$}.\end{cases}

(4.12)

The first inequality in (
4.12
) is a consequence of (
4.6
). In (
4.12
)
2
, as a constant
c
we can take

c=\|\tilde{a}(\cdot,T)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}(T^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(2-\gamma)/2}\leq\|g_{2}(\cdot,T)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}(T^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(2-\gamma)/2}
. This follows from (
4.4
) and again from (
4.11
).

4.2
The energy functions

These are two functions bound to quantify the minimal energy controlled by the functional in (
1.1
); they will play a crucial role in the rest of the paper. For every
(x,t)\in\Omega\times(0,\infty)
, we set

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle G(x,t):=\int_{T}^{\max\{t,T\}}g_{1}(x,s)s\,ds\\
\ \bar{G}(x,t):=G(x,t)+(T^{2}+1)^{\gamma/2}\,.\end{cases}

(4.13)

4.3
Quantifying nonuniform ellipticity

Here we quantify ellipticity and growth of
F
in the nonuniformly elliptic case. For this, we consider numbers
d,\sigma,\hat{\sigma}\geq 0
such that

h(\cdot)\in L^{d}(\Omega)\,,\qquad d>n

(4.14)

and

\sigma+\hat{\sigma}<\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\min\left\{\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{d},\frac{4}{\vartheta(n-2)}\left(1-\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)\right\}&\mbox{if}&n>2\\[8.0pt]
\min\left\{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{d},\frac{2}{\vartheta}\left(1-\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)\right\}&\mbox{if}&n=2\,.\end{array}\right.

(4.15)

Here it is
\vartheta\equiv\vartheta(\gamma)=1
when
\gamma\geq 2
, and
\vartheta=2
otherwise.
We assume that
x\mapsto g_{1}(x,t)\in W^{1,d}(\Omega)
for all
t\geq T
and that
\partial_{x}g_{1}(\cdot)
is Carathéodory regular on
\Omega\times[T,\infty)
. Then, for a fixed constant
c_{\textnormal{b}}\geq 1
, we consider the assumptions

\lvert\partial_{x}g_{1}(x,t)\rvert\leq h(x)[\bar{G}(x,t)]^{\hat{\sigma}}g_{1}(x,t)\,,

(4.16)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\ g_{3}(x,t)t\leq c_{\textnormal{b}}[\bar{G}(x,t)]^{1+\sigma}\\[9.0pt]
\displaystyle\ \frac{[g_{3}(x,t)]^{2}}{g_{1}(x,t)}\leq c_{\textnormal{b}}[\bar{G}(x,t)]^{1+2\sigma}\,,\end{array}\right.

(4.17)

\frac{g_{2}(x,t)}{g_{1}(x,t)}\leq c_{\textnormal{b}}[\bar{G}(x,t)]^{\sigma}

(4.18)

for every
t\geq T
, every
x\in\Omega
in the case of (
4.18
), and for a.e.
x\in\Omega
in the case of (
4.16
)-(
4.17
).
Comments on the meaning of (
4.16
)-(
4.18
) can be found in Section
4.6
below.

4.4
The uniformly elliptic case

Here we instead describe conditions relevant to the uniformly elliptic case, and therefore to models as in (
1.23
). Such conditions are a slightly more general version of those considered in
CM0
;
CM1
;
liebe1
, that are classical. We retain the structure conditions (
4.1
)-(
4.4
) from Section
4.1
but, instead of using (
4.14
)-(
4.18
) from Section
4.3
, for a fixed constant
c_{\textnormal{u}}\geq 1
this time we consider

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ t>0\Longrightarrow g_{i}(x,t)\equiv g_{i}(t),\ \ i\in\{1,2,3\}\\
\ T\leq t\Longrightarrow g_{2}(t)\leq c_{\textnormal{u}}g_{1}(t)\\
\ T\leq t\Longrightarrow g_{1}(t)t\leq g_{3}(t)\leq c_{\textnormal{u}}g_{1}(t)t\,.\end{cases}

(4.19)

4.5
General results

In the following, we abbreviate the assumptions considered as

\begin{cases}\texttt{set}:=\left\{(\ref{0ass})-(\ref{00}),(\ref{sigmad})-(\ref{9})\right\}\\
\texttt{set}_{\textnormal{u}}:=\left\{(\ref{0ass})-(\ref{00}),(\ref{uni0})\right\}\end{cases}

(4.20)

and these are going to be used in the nonuniformly elliptic setting, and in the uniformly elliptic one, respectively.
Accordingly, we also gather the parameters influencing the constants in the a priori estimates as

\begin{cases}\texttt{data}:=\left(n,N,\nu,\gamma,T,c_{\textnormal{b}},d,\sigma,\hat{\sigma},\mathfrak{a}\right)\\
\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{u}}:=\left(n,N,\nu,\gamma,T,c_{\textnormal{u}},g_{1}(1),\mathfrak{a}\right).\end{cases}

(4.21)

The presence of
\mathfrak{a}
only occurs in the two-dimensional case
n=2
.

Theorem 4.1

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1
), under assumptions
set
in
(
4.20
)
, with
f\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
as defined in
(
1.9
)
. Then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
. Moreover, there exists a positive radius
R_{*}\equiv R_{*}(\texttt{data},f(\cdot))\leq 1
such that if
\mathcal{B}\Subset\Omega
is a ball with
\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})\leq R_{*}
, then

\displaystyle\lVert G(\cdot,\lvert Du\rvert)\rVert_{L^{\infty}(s\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c}{(1-s)^{\beta}[\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{\beta}}\left[\|F(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}^{\theta}+\lVert f\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B})}^{\theta}+1\right]

(4.22)

holds for every
s\in(0,1)
, where
c\equiv c(\texttt{data},\lVert h\rVert_{L^{d}(\mathcal{B})})\geq 1
,
\beta,\theta\equiv\beta,\theta(n,\gamma,\linebreak d,\sigma,\hat{\sigma})>0
.

Remark 3

When either
\gamma\geq 2
or
f\equiv 0
, in Theorem
4.1
we can replace the upper bound on
\sigma+\hat{\sigma}
in (
4.15
) by the less restrictive
\sigma+\hat{\sigma}<1/n-1/d
. See Remark
9
below.

The main Lipschitz regularity result in the uniformly elliptic case is in the next

Theorem 4.2

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1
), under assumptions
\texttt{set}_{\textnormal{u}}
in
(
4.20
)
, and with
f,h\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
as defined in
(
1.9
)
. Then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
. Moreover, there exists a positive radius
R_{*}\equiv R_{*}(\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{u}},h(\cdot))\leq 1
such that if
\mathcal{B}\Subset\Omega
is a ball with
\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})\leq R_{*}
, then

\displaystyle\|F(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{\infty}(s\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c}{(1-s)^{n}[\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{n}}\|F(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}+c\|f\|_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B})}^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)}+c

(4.23)

holds for every
s\in(0,1)
, where
c\equiv c(\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{u}})
.

In deriving Theorem
4.1
we need to prove higher integrability bounds for the gradient, that are worth being singled out in the following:

Theorem 4.3

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1
), under assumptions
set
in
(
4.20
)
with
n>2
, and with
f\in L^{n}(\Omega)
. Then
Du\in L^{p}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
for every
p\in[1,\infty)
. Moreover, for every
p\in[1,\infty)
there exists a positive radius
R_{*}\equiv R_{*}(\texttt{data},f(\cdot),p)\leq 1
such that, if
\mathcal{B}\Subset\Omega
is a ball with
\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})\leq R_{*}
, then

\displaystyle\|G(\cdot,\lvert Du\rvert)\|_{L^{p}(s\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c}{(1-s)^{\beta_{p}}[\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{\beta_{p}}}\left[\|F(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}^{\theta_{p}}+\lVert f\rVert_{L^{n}(\mathcal{B})}^{\theta_{p}}+1\right]

(4.24)

holds for every
s\in(0,1)
, where
c\equiv c(\texttt{data},\lVert h\rVert_{L^{d}(\mathcal{B})},p)
,
\theta_{p},\beta_{p}\equiv\theta_{p},\beta_{p}(n,\linebreak\gamma,d,\sigma,\hat{\sigma},p)
.

Remark 4

In order to prove Theorem
4.3
the full strength of (
4.15
) is not actually needed. Assuming

\sigma+\hat{\sigma}<1/n-1/d
suffices.

Remark 5

In Theorems
4.1
-
4.3
, as well as in the other estimates in this paper, the constants depending on
data
and
\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{u}}
blow-up when
T\to\infty
(complete loss of ellipticity). On the contrary, they remain bounded when
T\to 0
(full recovery of ellipticity), as long as the quantity
\nu\equiv\nu(T)
in (
4.6
) stays bounded away from zero. This is for instance the case in Theorems
1.1
-
1.5
; see Section
10
below. As a matter of fact, there is an additional dependence of the constants on the specific operator considered. Indeed, the dependence on
T
typically shows up via quantities controlled by
\|\tilde{a}(\cdot,T)\|_{L^{\infty}}(T^{2}+\mu^{2})+T^{\gamma}
; this can be in turn controlled as
\lesssim\|\tilde{a}(\cdot,1)\|_{L^{\infty}}(T^{\gamma}+\mu^{\gamma})
for
T\leq 1
via (
4.4
). All in all, the dependence of the constants on the specific operator (i.e.,
\partial_{x}F
) appears only through
\nu
defined in (
4.6
), and
\|\tilde{a}(\cdot,1)\|_{L^{\infty}}
when
T
approaches zero. See also the appearance of
g_{1}(1)
in
\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{u}}
in the uniformly elliptic case.

Finally, we give another general result concerning an intermediate form of nonuniform ellipticity. This for instance applies to the case of functionals with special structure as the double phase one in (
1.10
). More comments on this will follow in Section
4.6
below.

Theorem 4.4

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1
), under assumptions
set
in
(
4.20
)
with
\sigma=\hat{\sigma}
in (
4.16
)-(
4.17
) and
f
as in
(
1.9
)
. Moreover, replace
(
4.15
)
and
(
4.18
)
by

\hat{\sigma}<\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}1/n-1/d&\mbox{if}&n>2\\[6.0pt]
\min\{1/2-1/d,\gamma-1\}&\mbox{if}&n=2\end{array}\right.\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{g_{2}(x,t)}{g_{1}(x,t)}\leq c_{\textnormal{b}}\,,

(4.25)

respectively. Then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
and
(
4.22
)
holds.

Remark 6

Replacing the integrand
F
in (
1.1
) with the new one
F_{0}(x,z):=F(x,z)-\tilde{F}(x,0)
, does not change the set of minimizers of
\mathcal{F}
, and gets another functional still satisfying the conditions of Theorems
4.1
-
4.4
. Moreover, as
\tilde{F}(x,0)\geq 0
, once the estimates in (
4.22
)-(
4.24
) are proved in the case
F_{0}(x,z)
is considered, then they also hold in the original case. Therefore for the rest of the paper we can always assume that
\tilde{F}(x,0)=0
and by (
4.7
)
it follows that

\displaystyle\tilde{F}(x,t)=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{a}(x,s)s\,ds\quad\mbox{for}\ (x,t)\in\Omega\times(0,\infty)\,.

(4.26)

Such a replacement makes sense provided
\tilde{F}(x,0)\in L^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
, but this is always the case in our situation. Specifically, this is true as soon as the functional
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1
) admits a minimizer
u
, which is therefore such that
\tilde{F}(\cdot,|Du|)\in L^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
. This implies that
0\leq\tilde{F}(x,0)\leq\tilde{F}(x,|Du(x)|)
(recall that
t\mapsto\tilde{F}(x,t)
is non-decreasing), so that
\tilde{F}(\cdot,0)\in L^{1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
.

4.6
Different notions of nonuniform ellipticity

What do we call nonuniform ellipticity of the integrand
F
here? To provide a measure, in the autonomous case
F(x,z)\equiv F(z)
, it is rather standard to use the ellipticity ratio

\mathcal{R}_{F}(z):=\frac{\mbox{highest eigenvalue of}\ \partial_{zz}F(z)}{\mbox{lowest eigenvalue of}\ \partial_{zz}F(z)}\,.

(4.27)

The occurrence of nonuniform ellipticity then refers to the fact that
\mathcal{R}_{F}(z)\to\infty
as
|z|\to\infty
; accordingly, the uniformly elliptic case occurs when
\mathcal{R}_{F}(\cdot)\approx 1
. In the nonautonomous case this leads to define the same pointwise quantity
\mathcal{R}_{F}(x,z)
, by taking into account the
x
-dependence in the right-hand side of (
4.27
). In this paper we have considered assumptions aimed at bounding
\mathcal{R}_{F}(x,z)
pointwise, i.e.,

\mathcal{R}_{F}(x,z)\lesssim\frac{g_{2}(x,|z|)}{g_{1}(x,|z|)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{9})}}{{\lesssim}}\left(\int^{|z|}_{T}g_{1}(x,s)s\,ds\right)^{\sigma}+1\,.

(4.28)

On the other hand, the ratio
\mathcal{R}_{F}(x,z)
misses to properly encode full information in the nonautonomous one. This leads to consider the nonlocal quantity

\mathfrak{R}_{F}(z,B):=\frac{\sup_{x\in B}\,\mbox{highest eigenvalue of}\ \partial_{zz}F(x,z)}{\inf_{x\in B}\,\mbox{lowest eigenvalue of}\ \partial_{zz}F(x,z)}\,,

(4.29)

with
B\subset\Omega
being any ball. The new ratio
\mathfrak{R}_{F}(z,B)
naturally occurs in integral estimates and, in fact, turns out to be the right quantity to describe intermediate notions of nonuniform ellipticity in the nonautonomous case.
Obviously, from the definitions it follows that

\sup_{x\in B}\,\mathcal{R}_{F}(x,z)\leq\mathfrak{R}_{F}(z,B)
, with equality in the autonomous case.

Remark 7
(Double face of the double phase functional (
1.10
))

To further motivate (
4.29
), recall that for general uniformly elliptic problems one typically recovers classical theories as Schauder’s
liebe1
and Calderón-Zygmund’s
BLO
. This is in general not the case for the double phase integral in (
1.10
), due to the counterexamples in
sharp
;
FMM
. Nevertheless the integrand
H(\cdot)
turns out to be uniformly elliptic when using the quantity in (
4.27
) as test, in the sense that
\mathcal{R}_{H}(\cdot)\approx 1
. On the other hand, when considering a ball
B
such that
B\cap\{a(x)=0\}\not=0
, we have that
\mathfrak{R}_{H}(z,B)\approx|B|^{1/n-1/d}|z|^{q-p}+1
, therefore
\mathfrak{R}_{H}(z,B)\to\infty
when
|z|\to\infty
. In such sense, the use of quantity in (
4.29
) resolves the above ambiguity. In this case, the assumptions (
1.19
) provide a way to correct the growth of
\mathfrak{R}_{H}(z,B)
with respect to
|z|
with the smallness of
|B|
, as pointed out in the Introduction.

In this paper we consider different assumptions, playing with the parameters
\sigma,\hat{\sigma}
in (
4.16
)-(
4.18
), in order to tune different degrees of nonuniform ellipticity, involving both
\mathcal{R}_{F}(\cdot)
and
\mathfrak{R}_{F}(\cdot)
. In Theorems
4.1
-
4.2
, we prescribe a direct pointwise bound on
\mathcal{R}_{F}(\cdot)
as in (
4.28
), and then control the behaviour with respect to
x
of the derivatives of
F
via (
4.16
)-(
4.17
). This has the overall effect of providing an indirect control on
\mathfrak{R}_{F}(\cdot)
too. In Theorem
4.4
, we bound
\mathcal{R}_{F}(\cdot)\lesssim 1
, that is, uniform ellipticity is assumed in the pointwise sense, still allowing for nonuniform ellipticity in the nonlocal sense of (
4.29
). This leads for instance to get better bounds and opens the way to Theorem
1.3
, dealing with the intermediate case of double phase functionals in the sense of Remark
7
.

5
Approximation of integrands

Here we implement a truncation scheme aimed at approximating the original integrand
F
in (
1.1
) with a family
\{F_{\varepsilon}\}
of integrands with standard polynomial growth, and converging to
F
locally uniformly. The new integrands preserve structure properties as (
4.1
)-(
4.2
), with corresponding control functions
g_{i,\varepsilon},
still satisfying relations as those of the original ones. For this we will exploit a few arguments used in
BM
as a starting point. In this section we shall permanently assume that (
4.1
)-(
4.4
) are in force, so that their consequences (
4.5
)-(
4.12
) can be used as well. Additional assumptions as (
4.16
)-(
4.18
) shall also be considered; in case, this will be explicitly mentioned. In the following we use a parameter
\varepsilon
that will always be such that
0<\varepsilon<\min\{1,T\}/4
and use that
\tilde{F}(x,0)\equiv 0
.

5.1
General setup

Given
T
and
\mu
in (
4.2
) and (
4.4
), respecively, we introduce the numbers

\displaystyle\mu_{\varepsilon}:=\mu+\varepsilon\,,\qquad T_{\varepsilon}:=T+1/\varepsilon\,.

(5.1)

With
\tilde{a}\colon\Omega\times(0,\infty)\mapsto[0,\infty)
that has been defined in (
1.3
) as

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(x,t):=\frac{\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)}{t}

(5.2)

for
t>0
, which is continuous on
\Omega\times(0,\infty)
, we start introducing the functions
\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}\colon\Omega\times[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
as

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,t):=\begin{cases}\ \frac{\tilde{a}(x,\varepsilon)}{(\varepsilon^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\varepsilon})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\varepsilon})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\ \ &\mbox{if}\ t\in[0,\varepsilon)\\
\ \tilde{a}(x,t)\ \ &\mbox{if}\ t\in[\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon})\\
\ \frac{\tilde{a}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\varepsilon})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\varepsilon})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\ \ &\mbox{if}\ t\in[T_{\varepsilon},\infty)\,,\end{cases}

(5.3)

for every
x\in\Omega
, where
\gamma
also appears in (
4.4
); accordingly, we define

\begin{cases}\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(x,z):=\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert),\ \mbox{for}\ \tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,s)s\,ds+\varepsilon L_{\gamma,\varepsilon}(t)\\
\displaystyle L_{\gamma,\varepsilon}(t):=\frac{1}{\gamma}\left[(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\gamma/2}-\mu_{\varepsilon}^{\gamma}\right]=\int_{0}^{t}(s^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}s\,ds\,,\end{cases}

(5.4)

and, finally

\displaystyle\bar{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,t):=\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)+\varepsilon(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\Longrightarrow a_{\varepsilon}(x,z):=\bar{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)z=\partial_{z}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\,.

(5.5)

Note that
\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}
is continuous on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)
and
\partial_{x}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}
is Carathéodory regular;
(x,t)\mapsto\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
is instead Borel regular.
In view of (
4.1
) and (
4.26
), it follows

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ t\mapsto\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\in C^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}[0,\infty)\cap C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}\left([0,\infty)\setminus\{\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon}\}\right)\ \ \mbox{for all}\ x\in\Omega\\
\ x\mapsto\tilde{F}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\in W^{1,n}(\Omega)\ \ \mbox{for all}\ \ t\in[0,\infty)\\
\ t\mapsto\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\in\textnormal{Lip}_{\textnormal{loc}}[0,\infty)\cap C^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}\left([0,\infty)\setminus\{\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon}\}\right)\ \ \mbox{for all}\ x\in\Omega\\
\ t\mapsto\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\ \ \mbox{is strictly convex and non-decreasing for all $x\in\Omega$}\\
\ F_{\varepsilon}\ \mbox{is continuous on $\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}$ and $\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x,0)=0$}\\
\ F_{\varepsilon}\to F\quad\mbox{uniformly on $\Omega\times\mathcal{K}$ as $\varepsilon\to 0$, $\forall\,\mbox{compact}\,\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}$ }\\
\ \mbox{$\partial_{x}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}$ is Carath\'{e}odory-regular on $\Omega\times[0,\infty)$}\,.\end{cases}

(5.6)

For (
5.6
)
4
, see (
5.17
) below.
For the proof of (
5.6
)
5
, recall that
\tilde{F}^{\prime}
is continuous on
\Omega\times(0,\infty)
.
For details about (
5.6
)
6
, see also Lemma
6
below. For (
5.6
)
7
use that
\partial_{x}\tilde{F}^{\prime}
is Carathéodory.

Definitions (
5.1
)-(
5.3
) lead to introduce new control functions
g_{i,\varepsilon}\colon\Omega\times[0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
as:

\displaystyle g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t):=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\begin{cases}\ \frac{g_{1}(x,\varepsilon)}{(\varepsilon^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\quad&\mbox{if}\ t\in[0,\varepsilon)\\
\ g_{1}(x,t)\quad&\mbox{if}\ t\in[\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon})\\
\ \frac{g_{1}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\quad&\mbox{if}\ t\in[T_{\varepsilon},\infty)\,,\end{cases}

(5.7)

\displaystyle g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,t):=\mathfrak{g}_{2}\begin{cases}\ \left[\frac{g_{2}(x,\varepsilon)}{(\varepsilon^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}+\varepsilon\right](t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\quad&\mbox{if}\ t\in[0,\varepsilon)\\
\ g_{2}(x,t)+\varepsilon(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\quad&\mbox{if}\ t\in[\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon})\\
\ \left[\frac{g_{2}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}+\varepsilon\right](t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\quad&\mbox{if}\ t\in[T_{\varepsilon},\infty)\,,\end{cases}

(5.8)

\displaystyle g_{3,\varepsilon}(x,t):=\begin{cases}\ \frac{g_{3}(x,\varepsilon)}{(\varepsilon^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\varepsilon}(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}t\quad&\mbox{if}\ t\in[0,\varepsilon)\\
\ g_{3}(x,t)\quad&\mbox{if}\ t\in[\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon})\\
\ \frac{g_{3}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}T_{\varepsilon}}(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}t\quad&\mbox{if}\ t\in[T_{\varepsilon},\infty)\,,\end{cases}

(5.9)

where the constants
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{1}:=\min\{1,\gamma-1\}\leq 1\leq\mathfrak{g}_{2}:=20(Nn+\gamma)\,.

(5.10)

Observe that
g_{1,\varepsilon},g_{2,\varepsilon}
are still continuous and
g_{3,\varepsilon}
is still Carathéodory regular. We next introduce also the truncated counterparts of the functions defined in (
4.13
), i.e.,

\begin{cases}\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}(x,t):=\int_{T}^{\max\{t,T\}}g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,s)s\,ds\\
\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t):=G_{\varepsilon}(x,t)+(T^{2}+1)^{\gamma/2}\,.\end{cases}

(5.11)

In the following, recalling (
5.1
), we shall repeatedly use

1\leq\frac{s^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{s^{2}+\mu^{2}}\leq 3,\ \mbox{provided $s\geq\varepsilon$}

(5.12)

and that this is a decreasing of
s
.

5.2
Four technical lemmas

Lemma 3

Under assumptions (
4.1
)-(
4.4
).

•

There exist constants
\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\}
and
\{L_{\varepsilon}\}
such that the following properties hold:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ \lvert\partial_{zz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\rvert\leq g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\ &\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\in\Omega\times\left\{\lvert z\rvert\geq T\,,\lvert z\rvert\not=T_{\varepsilon}\right\}\\
\ g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}\leq\partial_{zz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\xi\cdot\xi\ &\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\in\Omega\times\left\{\lvert z\rvert\geq T\,,\lvert z\rvert\not=T_{\varepsilon}\right\}\\
\ \lvert\partial_{xz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\rvert\leq h(x)g_{3,\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\ &\mbox{for a.e.\,$x\in\Omega$ and every $z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}$}\\
\ |\partial_{x}\bar{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)||z|\leq h(x)g_{3,\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\ &\mbox{for a.e.\,$x\in\Omega$ and every $z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}$}\end{cases}

(5.13)

and

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ \lvert\partial_{zz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\rvert\leq\Lambda_{\varepsilon}(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\ &\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\in\Omega\times\left\{\lvert z\rvert\not=\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon}\right\}\\
\ \varepsilon\mathfrak{g}_{1}(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}\leq\partial_{zz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\xi\cdot\xi\ &\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\in\Omega\times\left\{\lvert z\rvert\not=\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon}\right\}\\
\ \lvert\partial_{xz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\rvert\leq L_{\varepsilon}h(x)(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}|z|\ &\mbox{for a.e.\,$x\in\Omega,\ \forall\,z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}$}\end{cases}

(5.14)

for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
. As a consequence, it follows that

\displaystyle g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\leq\bar{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\leq g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)

(5.15)

holds for all
(x,z)\in\Omega\times\{\lvert z\rvert\geq T\}
, and

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ \bar{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)+\bar{a}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\lvert z\rvert\geq g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\\
\ \bar{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)+\bar{a}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\lvert z\rvert\leq g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\end{cases}

(5.16)

hold for all
(x,z)\in\Omega\times\{\lvert z\rvert\geq T,\ \lvert z\rvert\not=T_{\varepsilon}\}
.

•

It holds that

0<s\leq t\ \Longrightarrow\begin{cases}g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,s)s\leq g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)t\\
\ \tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,s)s\leq\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)t\,.\end{cases}

(5.17)

In particular, the function
t\mapsto G_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
in
(
5.11
)
is convex.

•

For every
(x,z)\in\Omega\times\{\lvert z\rvert\geq T\}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ c(\nu,\gamma)(|z|^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\leq g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,|z|)\\
\ G(x,\lvert z\rvert)\leq F(x,z)\\
G_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert z\rvert)\leq F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\,.\end{cases}

(5.18)

For every
(x,t)\in\Omega\times[T,\infty)
, there holds

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\gamma/2}\leq c(\nu,\gamma)\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\\
\displaystyle{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\leq c(\nu,\gamma)[\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)]^{2/\gamma}g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)\,.\end{array}\right.

(5.19)

Again for every
(x,z)\in\Omega\times\{\lvert z\rvert\geq T\}
, it holds

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ c(\nu,\gamma)F(x,z)\geq(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\gamma/2}-(T^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\gamma/2}\\
\ c(\nu,\gamma)F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\geq(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\gamma/2}-(T^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\gamma/2}\,.\end{cases}

(5.20)

•

For every
x\in\Omega
and for a fixed constant
c\equiv c(\nu,\gamma)
, we have

\begin{cases}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\leq c\tilde{a}(x,t)\quad\mbox{for $t\geq\varepsilon$}\\
(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\leq c\tilde{a}(x,t)\quad\mbox{for $t\geq T$}\,.\end{cases}

(5.21)

•

Finally, for another constant
c
depending on
\nu
and
\|\tilde{a}(\cdot,T)\|_{L^{\infty}}(T^{2}+\mu^{2})+T^{\gamma}
, we have that

F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\leq cF(x,z)+c\quad\mbox{holds for all }(x,z)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}

(5.22)

and that

\frac{\varepsilon}{\gamma}(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\gamma/2}-\frac{\varepsilon\mu_{\varepsilon}^{\gamma}}{\gamma}\leq\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\leq c_{\varepsilon}(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\gamma/2}

(5.23)

holds for all
(x,t)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty)
.

Proof

By the very definitions in (
5.3
)-(
5.4
), we note that
\partial_{zz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)
exists for all
(x,z)\in\Omega\times\left\{\lvert z\rvert\not=\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon}\right\}
with

\displaystyle\partial_{zz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)=\begin{cases}\ (\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\left[\frac{\tilde{a}(x,\varepsilon)}{(\varepsilon^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}+\varepsilon\right]\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}(z)\\
\qquad\quad\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\in\Omega\times\{\lvert z\rvert<\varepsilon\}\\
\ \partial_{zz}F(x,z)+\varepsilon(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}(z)\\
\qquad\quad\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\ \in\Omega\times\{\varepsilon<\lvert z\rvert<T_{\varepsilon}\}\\
\ (\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\left[\frac{\tilde{a}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}+\varepsilon\right]\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}(z)\\
\qquad\quad\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\in\Omega\times\{T_{\varepsilon}<\lvert z\rvert\}\,,\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}(z):=\mathbb{I}_{N\times n}+(\gamma-2)\frac{z\otimes z}{\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\quad\mbox{for}\ z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}\,.

Moreover, recalling (
5.3
), by the definition of weak derivatives we have

\displaystyle\partial_{xz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)=\begin{cases}\ \frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{a}(x,\varepsilon)}{(\varepsilon^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\varepsilon})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\varepsilon})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}z&\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\in\Omega\times\{\lvert z\rvert<\varepsilon\}\\
\ \partial_{x}\tilde{a}(x,|z|)z&\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\ \in\Omega\times\{\varepsilon\leq\lvert z\rvert<T_{\varepsilon}\}\\
\ \frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{a}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\varepsilon})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\varepsilon})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}z&\mbox{if}\ (x,z)\in\Omega\times\{T_{\varepsilon}\leq\lvert z\rvert\}\,.\end{cases}

Then (
5.13
)-(
5.14
) directly follow by (
4.2
), (
4.8
), (
5.3
), (
5.7
)-(
5.9
), and with the choice

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}:=\mathfrak{g}_{2}\left[1+\sup_{x\in\Omega}\frac{2g_{2}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}+\sup_{x\in\Omega,\lvert z\rvert\in[\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon}]}\frac{\lvert\partial_{zz}F(x,z)\rvert}{(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}\right],

\displaystyle L_{\varepsilon}:=\sup_{t\in[\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon}]}\frac{\|g_{3}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}}{(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}t}\,.

Note that we are also using (
4.11
)
2
and (
4.4
) in order to get upper bounds for
|\partial_{zz}F_{\varepsilon}(x,z)|
, in (
5.13
)
1
and (
5.14
)
1
, respectively; also (
5.12
) is used to estimate

\frac{\tilde{a}(x,\varepsilon)}{(\varepsilon^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}\leq\frac{2\tilde{a}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}\leq\frac{2g_{2}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}\,.

We also used (
4.10
)
2
to get (
5.13
)
2
.

As for (
5.15
)-(
5.16
), when
|z|\not=T_{\varepsilon}
, these follow from definition (
5.1
) and (
5.13
), arguing exactly as for (
4.10
)-(
4.11
). The case
|z|=T_{\varepsilon}
of (
5.15
) follows by continuity.

The property in (
5.17
) readily follows from (
4.5
) and the definitions in (
5.7
)-(
5.8
), also using the fact that
t\mapsto(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{(\gamma-2)/2}t
is increasing.

We now come to (
5.18
)-(
5.20
). For
\eqref{z6}_{2}
-
\eqref{z7}_{1}
we note that

\displaystyle F(x,z)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{T}^{\lvert z\rvert}\tilde{a}(x,s)s\,ds\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{22}_{2}}}{{\geq}}\int_{T}^{\lvert z\rvert}g_{1}(x,s)s\,ds\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{GG}}}{{=}}G(x,\lvert z\rvert)

(5.24)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{00bis}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\nu\int_{T}^{\lvert z\rvert}(s^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}s\,ds\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Hnot}}}{{=}}\frac{\nu}{\gamma}\left\{[E(\lvert z\rvert)]^{\gamma}-[E(T)]^{\gamma}\right\}.

Let us now take care of (
5.19
)
1
. For
t\in[T,T_{\varepsilon})
, using (
4.6
) and (
5.12
), we see that (recall that
\varepsilon\leq T/4
)

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{1}\nu}{\gamma}\left[(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\gamma/2}-(T^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\gamma/2}\right]+(T^{2}+1)^{\gamma/2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\gamma/2}}{c(\nu,\gamma)}\,,

(5.25)

while, when
t\geq T_{\varepsilon}
by analogous means, we have

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\int_{T_{\varepsilon}}^{t}\frac{g_{1}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}(s^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}s\,ds

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathfrak{g}_{1}\int_{T}^{T_{\varepsilon}}g_{1}(x,s)s\,ds+(T^{2}+1)^{\gamma/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ \ \geq\frac{1}{c(\nu,\gamma)}\int_{T}^{t}(s^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}s\,ds+(T^{2}+1)^{\gamma/2}\geq\frac{(t^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\gamma/2}}{c(\nu,\gamma)}\,.

(5.26)

Implicit in the arguments from (
5.25
)-(
5.26
) is also the proof of
\eqref{z6}_{1}
.
Inequalites
\eqref{z6}_{3}
and
\eqref{z7}_{2}
follow using the lower bound in (
5.15
) and then (
5.18
)
1
, as in (
5.24
). As for
\eqref{z10a}_{2}
, note that

\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=\int_{T}^{t}g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,s)s\,ds

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{einc})}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)t\int_{T}^{t}\,ds

(5.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)t(t-T)\leq g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)t^{2}

holds whenever
t\geq T
. Now
\eqref{z10a}_{2}
follows using (
5.27
) with
\eqref{z10a}_{1}
.

The proof of (
5.21
)
1
follows straightaway from the definition of
\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}
in (
5.3
), the first property in (
4.4
) and (
5.12
); the relation in (
5.21
)
2
instead follows from (
4.6
), (
4.10
)
2
and again (
5.12
).

For the proof of (
5.22
) we use (
4.6
) and (
5.12
), to get, in the case
t\leq T

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)

\displaystyle\leq c\left[\frac{\tilde{a}(x,T)}{(T^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}}+\varepsilon\right]\int_{0}^{t}(s^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}s\,ds

\displaystyle\leq c\|\tilde{a}(\cdot,T)\|_{L^{\infty}}(T^{2}+\mu^{2})+c(T^{\gamma}+1)\,.

(5.28)

On the other hand, when
t>T
, using (
5.21
) gives that
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)-\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(x,T)\leq c\tilde{F}(x,t)
and this, together with the content of the last display, gives (
5.21
) again. Finally, the proof of (
5.23
) follows straightaway from the definitions in (
5.3
)-(
5.4
).

Lemma 4

Under assumptions (
4.1
)-(
4.4
)

•

If (
4.18
) is also in force for some
\sigma\geq 0
, then for every
(x,t)\in\Omega\times[T,\infty)
it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)}\leq c[\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)]^{\sigma}\,,\quad\mbox{where $c\equiv c(n,N,\nu,\gamma,c_{\textnormal{b}})$}\,.

(5.29)

In particular, if
g_{2}/g_{2}\leq c_{\textnormal{b}}
holds as in
(
4.25
)
, then it also holds that

\displaystyle\frac{g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)}\leq c(n,N,\nu,\gamma,c_{\textnormal{b}})\,.

(5.30)

•

If (
4.16
) is also in force, then, with
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
as in
(
5.10
)
, for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
with
|z|\geq T
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lvert\partial_{x}g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)\rvert t\leq\frac{h(x)}{\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{\hat{\sigma}}(1+\hat{\sigma})}\partial_{t}[\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)]^{1+\hat{\sigma}}\,.

(5.31)

•

For all
(x,t)\in\Omega\times[T,\infty)
, the following holds:

\varepsilon_{1}<\varepsilon_{2}<\min\{1,T\}/4\Longrightarrow g_{1,\varepsilon_{2}}(x,t)\leq c(\gamma)g_{1,\varepsilon_{1}}(x,t)

(5.32)

so that, this time for all
(x,t)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty)
, it follows

G_{\varepsilon_{2}}(x,t)\leq c(\gamma)G_{\varepsilon_{1}}(x,t)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\,G_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=\mathfrak{g}_{1}G(x,t)\,.

(5.33)

The last convergence occurs uniformly on compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
.

Proof

For (
5.29
), note that (
4.6
), (
4.12
)
1
and (
5.12
) imply

T\leq t\leq T_{\varepsilon}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}\frac{g_{2}(x,t)}{g_{1}(x,t)}\leq\frac{g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)}\leq c(\nu,\gamma)\,\frac{g_{2}(x,t)}{g_{1}(x,t)}

(5.34)

and that the definitions in (
5.7
)-(
5.8
) imply

t>T_{\varepsilon}\Longrightarrow\frac{g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)}=\frac{g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}\,.

(5.35)

Then, for

T\leq t\leq T_{\varepsilon}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ccrrss}}}{{\leq}}c\frac{g_{2}(x,t)}{g_{1}(x,t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{9}}}{{\leq}}c[\bar{G}(x,t)]^{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{g1e})}}{{\leq}}c[\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)]^{\sigma}

(5.36)

while, for
T_{\varepsilon}<t
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,t)}{g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{ccrrss1})}}{{=}}\frac{g_{2,\varepsilon}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}{g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,T_{\varepsilon})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{banal}}}{{\leq}}[\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,T_{\varepsilon})]^{\sigma}\leq c[\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)]^{\sigma}\,,

and (
5.29
) is completely proved.
The proof of (
5.30
) follows as in the last two displays by formally taking
\sigma=0
. The proof of (
5.31
