)). The rest of the proof again follows with minor modifications to the proof for Theorem
4.1
.

9
Proof of Theorem
4.4

We revisit the proof of Theorem
4.1
starting from the part concerning the a priori estimates of Section
6
in the case
n>2
. In turn, this uses as a preliminary result Proposition
1
for
s_{*}=1
, that now works only assuming
\hat{\sigma}<1/n-1/d
, as allowed by (
4.25
). This relies on the condition
g_{2}/g_{1}\leq c_{\textnormal{b}}
in (
4.25
), that via (
5.29
) also implies
g_{2,\varepsilon}/g_{1,\varepsilon}\leq c
. Then Remark
8
can be used and we can replace
\sigma+\hat{\sigma}
by
\hat{\sigma}
in (
6.15
). The same arguments of Remark
8
also apply in the proof of Lemma
8
, and yield the following simplified form of (
8
):

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r/2}(x_{0})}\lvert D(G_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert Du\rvert)-\kappa)_{+}\rvert^{2}\ \,dx\leq\frac{c}{r^{2}}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}(G_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert Du\rvert)-\kappa)_{+}^{2}\ \,dx

\displaystyle\hskip 125.19212pt+c\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}[h(x)]^{2}[\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert Du\rvert)]^{2(1+\hat{\sigma})}\,dx

\displaystyle\hskip 125.19212pt+c\lVert Du\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r}(x_{0}))}^{2}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\lvert f\rvert^{2}\ \,dx\,.

This last inequality still holds when
n=2
. Using it as in Lemma
9
yields

\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}(x_{0},\lvert Du(x_{0})\rvert)

\displaystyle\leq\kappa+c\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})}(G_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert Du\rvert)-\kappa)_{+}^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ \ +c\left[\mathbf{P}_{1}^{\mathfrak{h}}(x_{0},2r_{0})+\lVert Du\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r_{0}}(x_{0}))}\mathbf{P}_{1}^{f}(x_{0},2r_{0})\right]\,,

(9.1)

for every
\kappa\geq 0
, which is in fact the analog of (
9
) in the present setting. Here it is
\mathfrak{h}(x):=h(x)[\bar{G}_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert Du\rvert)]^{1+\hat{\sigma}}
. From this last inequality we again arrive at (
3
) exactly as in the proof of
Proposition
3
(needless to say, for different exponents
\theta
and
\beta
). Here the key observation is that we do not have to verify the two conditions in (
6.70
), that in fact are not occurring (actually they occur taking formally
\sigma=0
). It is only the second, and more restrictive one, that requires the stronger bound in (
4.15
), that can therefore be relaxed in (
4.25
). Starting from this fact, the rest of the proof is the same as the one of Theorem
4.1
in the case
n>2
, again replacing
\sigma+\hat{\sigma}
by
\hat{\sigma}
. In the case
n=2
the argument is similar. First, we use Lemma
10
, again with
s_{*}=1
, and, as for the case
n>2
, this works replacing
\sigma+\hat{\sigma}
by
\hat{\sigma}
in (
6.74
). Next, we apply this time (
9
), thereby getting (
6.81
) (with formally
\sigma=0
) and then (
6.82
), with
\lVert G_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\lvert Du\rvert)\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\tau_{2}})}
replaced by
1
. From this point on, the proof goes as in the previous case; the only remark is that now the first condition in (
6.83
) does not appear as a consequence of the new version of (
6.82
), and this new bound is exactly the one appearing in (
4.25
) upon considering only
\hat{\sigma}
.

10
Proof of Theorems
1.1
-
1.7

10.1
Theorems
1.1
and
1.2

We just verify that the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
imply those of Theorem
4.1
for suitable choices of objects and parameters. For this we take
\gamma=p
,
g_{i}(x,t)\equiv g_{i}(t)
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, where
g_{1}(t):=\nu(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(p-2)/2}
,
g_{2}(t):=\Lambda(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(q-2)/2}+\Lambda(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(p-2)/2}
and
g_{3}(t):=(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(q-2)/2}t+(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(p-2)/2}t
,
for all
t\in(0,\infty)
. It follows that
G(x,t)\equiv G(t)=\nu[(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{p/2}-(T^{2}+\mu^{2})^{p/2}]/p
(for
t\geq T
and it is zero otherwise) and
\bar{G}(x,t)\equiv\bar{G}(t)=G(t)+(T^{2}+1)^{p/2}\geq\nu(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{p/2}+(p-\nu)/p
.
Here, any number
T\in(0,\infty)
is fine. By (
1.13
)-(
1.15
) we see that
F
satisfies (
4.1
)-(
4.4
). Note that
(
4.16
)
holds with
\hat{\sigma}=0
since
g_{1}
is
x
-independent. As for (
4.17
)-(
4.18
), we verify them choosing
\sigma=q/p-1
, for a suitably large constant
c_{\textnormal{b}}\equiv c_{\textnormal{b}}(n,N,\nu,p,q,\Lambda)\geq 1
. With such a choice of
\sigma
, and
\hat{\sigma}=0
as above, the condition in (
4.15
) is implied by the assumed one in (
1.16
). All in all, assumptions (
4.14
)-(
4.18
) are verified too and and we can apply Theorem
4.1
to get Theorem
1.1
. Finally, the last assertion concerning the improved bound in (
1.17
) follows from the content of Remark
3
and this completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Theorem
1.2
is a special case of Theorem
1.1
, upon taking
h(\cdot)\approx|D\mathfrak{c}(\cdot)|
. For this, just observe that the convexity of
z\to F(z)
, and the growth assumptions (
1.13
), imply also that
\tilde{F}^{\prime}(t)\lesssim(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(q-2)/2}t+(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(p-2)/2}t
, thereby allowing to verify also (
1.15
) for the integrand
(x,t)\mapsto\mathfrak{c}(x)\tilde{F}(t)
.

10.2
Theorem
1.3

As all our estimates are local in nature, we can assume that
H(\cdot,Du)\in L^{1}(\Omega)
, where the integrand
H(\cdot)
has been defined in (
1.10
); moreover, by localizing Morrey’s embedding theorem, we can also assume that
[a]_{0,1-n/d;\Omega}+\lVert a\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
is finite. Similarly to (
4.21
), we denote

\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{h}}:=(n,\nu,N,p,q,d,[a]_{0,1-n/d;\Omega},\lVert a\rVert_{L^{\infty}},\lVert H(\cdot,Du)\rVert_{L^{1}},\lVert f\rVert_{L^{n}})\,.

(10.1)

Here
[a]_{0,1-n/d;\Omega}
denotes the usual
(1-n/d)
-Hölder seminorm of
a(\cdot)
on
\Omega
, and all the norms extend over
\Omega
. The proof now proceeds in three steps.

Step 1: Quantification of ellipticity

We refer to the framework of Theorem
4.4
. We define, for
x\in\Omega
and
t>0
,

g_{1}(x,t):=\min\{p-1,1\}[t^{p-2}+a(x)t^{q-2}]
,
g_{2}(x,t):=2qNn[t^{p-2}+a(x)t^{q-2}]
,
g_{3}(x,t)\equiv g_{3}(t):=t^{q-1}

that implies

G(x,t):=\min\{p-1,1\}[(t^{p}/p+a(x)t^{q}/q)-(1/p+a(x)/q)]
(here we take
T=1
and
\mu=0
),
\bar{G}(x,t)=G(x,t)+2^{p/2}
and
g_{2}/g_{1}\leq c_{\rm b}
for
t\geq 1
, where
c_{\rm b}\equiv c_{\rm b}(\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{h}})
. We also set
h(\cdot)=\lvert Da(\cdot)\rvert
,
\sigma=\hat{\sigma}:=q/p-1
,
\gamma=p
. We now observe that, replacing
\leq
by
<
in the first bound from (
1.19
), we can immediately conclude with the local Lipschitz continuity of minima invoking Theorem
4.4
, whose assumptions, and in particular (
4.25
), are satisfied but in the limiting/equality case. In order to get the delicate equality in (
1.19
), we have to readapt some points from the proof of Theorem
4.4
using some additional results available when the peculiar structure in (
1.10
) is considered.

Step 2: Uniform higher integrability

We modify and extend arguments from
CoM0
;
CoM
;
demicz
.

Lemma 12

Let
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of the functional in (
1.10
), under assumptions (
1.2
) and
0\leq a(\cdot)\in W^{1,d}(\Omega)
with
(
1.17
)
. There exists
s_{**}\equiv s_{**}(\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{h}})>1
such that
H(\cdot,Du)\in L^{s_{**}}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
.

Proof

Consider concentric balls
B_{\varrho}\subset B_{t}\Subset B_{s}\subset B_{R}
contained in
\Omega
, then take a standard cut-off function
\eta\in C^{1}_{{\rm c}}(B_{R})
so that
\mathds{1}_{B_{t}}\leq\eta\leq\mathds{1}_{B_{s}}
and
\lvert D\eta\rvert\lesssim 1/(s-t)
. We take
w:=u-\eta(u-(u)_{B_{R}})
as competitor to minimality, that yields

\|H(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{1}(B_{s})}\leq\|H(\cdot,Dw)\|_{L^{1}(B_{s})}+\|\eta f\cdot(u-(u)_{B_{R}})\|_{L^{1}(B_{s})}
. The first term in the right-hand side of this last inequality can be handled as in
(
CoM0
, Section 9)
, while by Sobolev and Young inequalities we have

\displaystyle\|\eta f\cdot(u-(u)_{B_{R}})\|_{L^{1}(B_{s})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{n}(B_{s})}\|\eta(u-(u)_{B_{R}})\|_{L^{n/(n-1)}(B_{s})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq cR^{n-n/p}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{n}(\Omega)}\lVert Du\rVert_{L^{p}(B_{s})}+\frac{cR^{n-n/p}}{s-t}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{n}(\Omega)}\|u-(u)_{B_{R}}\|_{L^{p}(B_{s})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{4}\|H(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{1}(B_{s})}+c\int_{B_{R}}\tilde{H}\left(x,\frac{|u-(u)_{B_{R}}|}{s-t}\right)\,dx+cR^{n}\,,

(10.2)

where

c\equiv c(n,p,q,\lVert f\rVert_{L^{n}(B)})
. This last estimate together with the arguments developed in
(
CoM0
, Section 9)
, leads to the reverse Hölder inequality

\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{R/2}}[H(\cdot,Du)]^{1+\delta}\,dx\right)^{1/(1+\delta)}\leq c\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B_{R}}[H(\cdot,Du)]\,dx+c\,,

for
c\equiv c(\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{h}})\geq 1
,
\delta\equiv\delta(\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{h}})>0
, which in turn allows us to conclude via a covering argument.

Let
B\Subset\Omega
be a ball with
\texttt{r}(B)\leq 1
. As is
CoM0
;
CoM
, we say that the
p
-phase occurs when

a_{\textnormal{i}}(B):=\inf_{x\in B}a(x)\leq 4[\texttt{r}(B)]^{1-n/d}[a]_{0,1-n/d;B}

(10.3)

holds, otherwise we say that the
(p,q)
-phase occurs. Accordingly, we define the integrand

\tilde{H}^{-}_{B}(|z|):=\lvert z\rvert^{p}/p+a_{\textnormal{i}}(B)\lvert z\rvert^{q}/q.

Finally, let
\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
,
\tilde{H}^{-}_{B,\varepsilon}(\cdot)
be the integrands defined by applying the construction in Section
5.1
, (
5.3
)-(
5.4
), to
\tilde{H}(\cdot)
and
\tilde{H}^{-}_{B}(\cdot)
, respectively, with
\gamma=p
and
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
defined in
Step 1
and
T=1
. Such a construction provides us with approximating integrands that are still of double phase type, i.e.,

\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{h}_{\varepsilon}(x,s)s\,ds+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}(s^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}s\,ds\,,

(10.4)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{\varepsilon}(x,t):=\begin{cases}\ \frac{\varepsilon^{p-2}}{(2\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}(t^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\\
\qquad+a(x)\frac{\varepsilon^{q-2}}{(2\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}(t^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\ \ &\mbox{if}\ t\in[0,\varepsilon)\\
\ t^{p-2}+a(x)t^{q-2}\ \ &\mbox{if}\ t\in[\varepsilon,T_{\varepsilon})\\
\ \frac{T_{\varepsilon}^{p-2}}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}(t^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\\
\qquad+a(x)\frac{T_{\varepsilon}^{q-2}}{(T_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}(t^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\ \ &\mbox{if}\ t\in[T_{\varepsilon},\infty)\,,\end{cases}

(10.5)

with the same representation holding for
\tilde{H}^{-}_{B,\varepsilon}(\cdot)
, replacing
a(\cdot)
by
a_{\textnormal{i}}(B)
in (
10.5
) above; here it is
T_{\varepsilon}=1+1/\varepsilon
.
Next, we develop an intrinsic Sobolev-Poincaré inequality involving
H_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
; the main point is that the implied constants and exponents are independent of
\varepsilon
.

Lemma 13

Assume that
0\leq a(\cdot)\in W^{1,d}(\Omega)
with
(
1.17
)
. Let
B\Subset\Omega
be a ball with
\texttt{r}(B)\leq 1
and
w\in W^{1,p}_{0}(B;\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
v\in W^{1,p}(B;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be such that
H_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,Dw),H_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,Dv)\in L^{1}(B)
. Then, the following Sobolev and Sobolev-Poincaré inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}\left(x,\frac{|w|}{\texttt{r}(B)}\right)\,dx\leq c\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}[\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(x,|Dw|)+1]^{\tau}\,dx\right)^{1/\tau}

(10.6)

c\equiv c(n,N,p,q,d,[a]_{0,1-n/d;B},\lVert Dw\rVert_{L^{p}(B)})
and

\displaystyle\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}\left(x,\frac{|v-(v)_{B}|}{\texttt{r}(B)}\right)\,dx\leq c\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}[\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(x,|Dv|)+1]^{\tau}\,dx\right)^{1/\tau}\,,

(10.7)

for
c\equiv c(n,N,p,q,d,[a]_{0,1-n/d;B},\lVert Dv\rVert_{L^{p}(B)})
. In both cases it is
\tau\equiv\tau(n,p,q)\linebreak\in(0,1)
.

Proof

We prove (
10.6
), the proof of (
10.7
) being totally similar. We start considering the
p
-phase; this is when (
10.3
) occurs. In this case, recalling
(
10.4
)
-
(
10.5
)
, we bound

\displaystyle\int_{B}\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}\left(x,\frac{|w|}{\texttt{r}(B)}\right)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c\varepsilon^{p}[\texttt{r}(B)]^{n}+c\int_{B\cap\{\varepsilon\leq\lvert w\rvert/\texttt{r}(B)<T_{\varepsilon}\}}\left(\int_{\varepsilon}^{\lvert w\rvert/\texttt{r}(B)}g_{1}(x,s)s\,ds\right)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+c\int_{B\cap\{\lvert w\rvert/\texttt{r}(B)\geq T_{\varepsilon}\}}\left(\int_{\varepsilon}^{\lvert w\rvert/\texttt{r}(B)}g_{1,\varepsilon}(x,s)s\,ds\right)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+c\varepsilon[\texttt{r}(B)]^{n}\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}\lvert Dw\rvert^{p_{*}}\,dx\right)^{p/p_{*}}\ \big(=:\mbox{(I)}+\mbox{(II)}+\mbox{(III)}++\mbox{(IV)}\big)\,,

where
c\equiv c(n,p,[a]_{0,1-n/d;B})
.
Now, note that the assumed bound in (
1.17
) implies
p_{*}\leq q_{*}<p
, where

b_{*}:=\max\,\{1,nb/(n+b)\}
, for
b\in\{p,q\}
.
Therefore using again Sobolev-Poincaré and Hölder’s inequalities, we estimate

\displaystyle\mbox{(II)}+\mbox{(III)}\leq c\int_{B}[(|w|/\texttt{r}(B))^{p}+a(x)(|w|/\texttt{r}(B))^{q}]\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+c\int_{B\cap\{\lvert w\rvert/\texttt{r}(B)\geq T_{\varepsilon}\}}[T_{\varepsilon}^{p}+a(x)T_{\varepsilon}^{q}+a(x)T_{\varepsilon}^{q-p}(|w|/\texttt{r}(B))^{p}]\,dx

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{pphase})}}{{\leq}}c[\texttt{r}(B)]^{n}\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}[(|w|/\texttt{r}(B))^{p}+[\texttt{r}(B)]^{1-n/d}(|w|/\texttt{r}(B))^{q}]\,dx

\displaystyle\ \leq c[\texttt{r}(B)]^{n}\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}\lvert Dw\rvert^{p_{*}}\,dx\right)^{p/p_{*}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+c[\texttt{r}(B)]^{n+1-n/d}\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}\lvert Dw\rvert^{p}\,dx\right)^{q/p-1}\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}\lvert Dw\rvert^{p(q_{*}/p)}\,dx\right)^{p/q_{*}}

\displaystyle\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{lox2bis})}}{{\leq}}c[\texttt{r}(B)]^{n}\left(\lVert Dw\rVert_{L^{p}(B)}^{q-p}+1\right)\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}\lvert Dw\rvert^{p\tilde{\tau}}\,dx\right)^{1/\tilde{\tau}},

with
c\equiv c(n,N,p,q,d,[a]_{0,1-n/d;B})
and
\tilde{\tau}:=q_{*}/p<1
. Merging the content of the last two displays above easily yields

\displaystyle\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}\left(x,\frac{|w|}{\texttt{r}(B)}\right)\,dx\leq c\left(\mathop{\int\hskip-10.50005pt-\,\!\!\!}\nolimits_{B}[\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(x,|Dw|)+1]^{\tilde{\tau}}\,dx\right)^{1/\tilde{\tau}}

(10.8)

for
c\equiv c(n,N,p,q,d,[a]_{0,1-n/d;B}\lVert Dw\rVert_{L^{p}(B)}^{q-p})
. We then pass to the
(p,q)
-phase, that is, when the complementary condition to (
10.3
) holds; here we follow the arguments from
(
CoM
, Section 4)
. It is then easy to see that
\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\sim\tilde{H}^{-}_{B,\varepsilon}(z)
for
(x,z)\in B\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
. Moreover, recalling that
H^{-}_{B,\varepsilon}
can be written as in (
10.4
)-(
10.5
) with
a(\cdot)
replaced by
a_{\textnormal{i}}(B)
, it follows that
\tilde{H}^{-}_{B,\varepsilon}
satisfies both
\Delta_{2}
and
\nabla_{2}
conditions (with constants depending on
n,p,q
, but independent of
\varepsilon
). We are therefore in position to argue as in the proof of
(
DiE
, Theorem 7)
, thereby again arriving at (
10.8
), for a different exponent
\tilde{\tau}\equiv\tau(n,p,q)\in(0,1)
. At this stage the proof of (
10.6
) follows merging the inequalities found in the two cases, and taking the largest of the two exponents
\tilde{\tau}
found for the two phases.

Given the inequalities in (
10.6
)-(
10.7
), and the representation in (
10.4
)-(
10.5
), we can proceed for instance as in
(
demicz
, Lemma 5)
to get a global gradient integrability result; this also involves estimates as in (
Proof
) to treat the additional
f
-terms appearing here with respect to the case considered in
demicz
. This involves a matching of local and up-to-the-boundary versions of Gehring’s lemma (see
KSt
).

Lemma 14

Let
\mathcal{B}\Subset\Omega
be a ball with
\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})\leq 1
. Under assumptions (
1.2
), and
0\leq a(\cdot)\in W^{1,d}(\Omega)
with
(
1.17
)
, let
u_{\varepsilon}\in W^{1,p}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{N})
solve

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}\mapsto\min_{w}\int_{\mathcal{B}}[\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(x,\lvert Dw\rvert)-f\cdot w]\,dx\qquad w\in u+W^{1,p}_{0}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{N})\,,

(10.9)

where
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
is a minimizer of the functional in (
1.10
).
Then there exists
s_{*}\in(1,s_{**})
, depending only on
\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{h}}
, but not on
\varepsilon
, such that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,|Du_{\varepsilon}|)+1\|_{L^{s_{*}}(\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\leq c\|\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,|Du|)+1\|_{L^{s_{*}}(\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\leq c\|\tilde{H}(\cdot,|Du|)+1\|_{L^{s_{*}}(\mathcal{B})}

(10.10)

holds for a constant
c\equiv c(\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{h}})
, where
s_{**}
is the exponent coming from Lemma
12
.

We finally remark that the constants appearing in (
10.10
) should a priori depend also on
\lVert Du_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{L^{p}(B)}
, as Lemma
13
is involved in the derivation of Lemma
14
(see again
demicz
), and it is used with the choices
w\equiv u_{\varepsilon}
and
v\equiv u_{\varepsilon}
. Such a dependence on
\varepsilon
does not actually occur; indeed, adapting estimate (
7
) to the present case leads to bounds as
\lVert Du_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathcal{B})}^{p}\lesssim\|H(\cdot,Du)+1\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}+[\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{n}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{n}(\mathcal{B})}^{p/(p-1)}
, thereby reducing the dependence of the constants on
\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{h}}
in (
10.1
). This fact is indeed used to prove the intermediate last inequality in (
10.10
) with an
\varepsilon
-independent constant
c
. The last inequality in (
10.10
) is instead a direct consequence of (
5.22
). In turn, the last quantity in (
10.10
) is finite by Lemma
12
.

Step 3: Completion of the proof of Theorem
1.3

We proceed almost verbatim as in the proof of Theorem
4.4
. We start with the case
n>2
; the only difference here is that we apply Proposition
1
with
s_{*}>1
being equal to the higher integrability exponent found in Lemma
14
. With no loss of generality we may assume that
s_{*}<\min\{2m(1+\hat{\sigma}),2^{*}\}=\min\{2mq/p,2^{*}\}
, as required in (
6.15
) (applied with
\sigma=0
). Note that this is possible when
\hat{\sigma}<s_{*}/n-s_{*}/d
and therefore, in particular, when

\hat{\sigma}\leq 1/n-1/d
, which is the case considered here. Indeed, with the choice made in Step 1, this last condition translates in
q/p\leq 1+1/n-1/d
, which is (
1.19
) for
n>2
. This is the essential point where the higher integrability estimates of Step 2 come into the play, allowing for equality in (
1.19
). We then proceed exactly as for Theorem
4.4
, as its assumptions are verified but for the equality case in (
1.19
), as noticed in Step 1. Proceeding as in the proof of Theorem
4.4
we arrive at (
9
), and all the foregoing considerations remain the same, but, in order to get a suitable a priori estimate, the term involving
\mathbf{P}_{1}^{\mathfrak{h}}
must be estimated slightly differently from Proposition
3
. More precisely, using (
6.16
) with the current choice of
s_{*}
in (
6.73
), we finally come to the new uniform bound

\displaystyle\lVert G_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\lvert Du_{\varepsilon}\rvert)\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\varsigma})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c}{(\varrho-\varsigma)^{\beta}}\left[\|\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,|Du_{\varepsilon}|)\|_{L^{s_{*}}(B_{\varrho})}^{\theta}+\lVert f\rVert_{L(n,1)(B_{\varrho})}^{\theta}+1\right]\,,

(10.11)

which replaces (
3
) and holds whenever
B_{\varsigma}\Subset B_{\varrho}
are concentric balls contained in
\mathcal{B}
; the constant
c
depends on
\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{h}}
. Note that we have applied the argument of Proposition
3
directly to
u_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
10.9
). Using (
10.10
) in (
10.11
) yields

\displaystyle\|G_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\lvert Du_{\varepsilon}\rvert)\|_{L^{\infty}(s\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c}{(1-s)^{\beta}[\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{\beta}}\left[\|H(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{s_{*}}(\mathcal{B})}^{\theta}+\lVert f\rVert_{L(n,1)(\mathcal{B})}^{\theta}+1\right]

for every
s\in(0,1)
. Again, the right-hand side stays finite by Lemma
12
. This last estimate can be used as a replacement of (
7
) in an approximation argument which is at this stage completely similar to the one used for the proof of Theorem
4.1
and this completes the proof in the case
n>2
. It remains to treat the case
n=2
. For this we again turn to the arguments of Section
6.7
, where we apply Lemma
10
with the choice of the number
s_{*}>1
again determined in Lemma
14
. Such an application is legal as we are assuming that
\hat{\sigma}=q/p-1\leq 1/2-1/d
, while again we may assume that
s_{*}<2m(1+\hat{\sigma})
. Then we proceed exactly as in the proof of Theorem
4.4
case
n=2
, again noting that only the second inequality in (
6.83
) is needed (formally with
\sigma=0
), and this leads to require that
q/p<p
, which is the second condition in (
1.19
) for the case
n=2
.

10.3
Proof of Theorems
1.4
-
1.5
and Theorem
1.7

We deduce Theorem
1.5
, from Theorem
4.1
; Theorem
1.4
then follows similarly. In exactly the same way, we can then deduce Theorem
1.7
from Theorem
4.3
. As usual, we do it by making a suitable choice of the growth functions
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
and of the parameters
\sigma,\hat{\sigma},c_{\textnormal{a}},c_{\textnormal{b}},\gamma,T,\mu
. This requires some preparations; we split the proof in two steps. In the following we denote
\texttt{data}_{k}\equiv(n,N,k,\nu_{\mathfrak{m}},L,p_{\mathfrak{m}},p_{m},p_{M})
, for every integer
k\geq 0
. In the following, with abuse of notation, we shall indicate by
\partial_{zz}{\bf e}_{k}(\cdot)
the Hessian matrix of
z\to{\bf e}_{k}(\cdot,|z|)
, while
{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)
keeps on denoting the (partial) derivative of the function
t\to{\bf e}_{k}(\cdot,t)
. By Sobolev-Morrey embedding theorem we can assume that
\{\mathfrak{c}_{k},p_{k}\}
are Hölder continuous functions with exponent
\alpha=1-n/d
.

Step 1: Computations

By induction we have

\displaystyle{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)=\mathfrak{c}_{k}(x)p_{k}(x)t^{p_{0}(x)-1}\Pi_{k}(x,t){\bf e}_{k}(x,t)\ \ \mbox{for}\ \ k\geq 0\,,

(10.12)

for every
x\in\Omega
and
t>0
, where

\begin{cases}\Pi_{k}(x,t):=\mathfrak{c}_{k-1}(x)p_{k-1}(x)[{\bf e}_{k-1}(x,t)]^{p_{k}(x)}\Pi_{k-1}(x,t)\ \ \mbox{for $k\geq 1$}\\
\Pi_{0}(x,t):=1\,.\end{cases}

Then, observing that

\Pi_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)=\Pi_{k}(x,t)t^{p_{0}(x)-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{j+1}(x)\mathfrak{c}_{j}(x)p_{j}(x)\Pi_{j}(x,t)\ \ \mbox{for}\ \ k\geq 1\,,

identity (
10.12
) gives

{\bf e}_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x,t)=t^{p_{0}(x)-1}{\bf e}_{0}^{\prime}(x,t)\left[\mathfrak{c}_{0}(x)p_{0}(x)+\frac{p_{0}(x)-1}{t^{p_{0}(x)}}\right]

and

\displaystyle{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime\prime}(x,t)

\displaystyle\,=t^{p_{0}(x)-1}{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)\mathfrak{c}_{k}(x)p_{k}(x)\Pi_{k}(x,t)

\displaystyle\qquad+t^{p_{0}(x)-1}{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{j+1}(x)\mathfrak{c}_{j}(x)p_{j}(x)\Pi_{j}(x,t)

\displaystyle\qquad+t^{p_{0}(x)-1}{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)\frac{p_{0}(x)-1}{t^{p_{0}(x)}}\qquad\mbox{for}\ \ k\geq 1\,.

(10.13)

Finally, it is

[{[\bf e}_{k}(x,t)]^{p_{k+1}(x)}]^{\prime}=[{\bf e}_{k}(x,t)]^{p_{k+1}(x)}p_{k+1}(x)\mathfrak{c}_{k}(x)p_{k}(x)t^{p_{0}(x)-1}\Pi_{k}(x,t)\,.

We now come to the
x
-derivatives; note that the following computations are justified as in (
1.21
) we are considering a composition of bounded Sobolev functions
\mathfrak{c}_{k},p_{k}
with smooth functions, and therefore the standard (vectorial) chain rule applies as in the traditional case, i.e., if
\mathfrak{c}_{k},p_{k}
were
C^{1}
-regular. We use properly defined, auxiliary vector fields
\mathcal{D}_{k},\mathcal{L}_{k}\colon\Omega\times(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
k\geq 0
, and the notation
{\bf e}_{-1}(x,t)\equiv t
. Then, we have, for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and
t>0

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{x}{\bf e}_{k}(x,t)={\bf e}_{k}(x,t)[{\bf e}_{k-1}(x,t)]^{p_{k}(x)}\mathcal{D}_{k}(x,t)\,,\quad\mbox{for}\ k\geq 0\\
\partial_{x}[{\bf e}_{k-1}(x,t)]^{p_{k}(x,t)}:=[{\bf e}_{k-1}(x,t)]^{p_{k}(x,t)}[{\bf e}_{k-2}(x,t)]^{p_{k-1}(x,t)}\\
\hskip 102.42992pt\times\left[\mathfrak{c}_{k-1}(x)Dp_{k}(x)+p_{k}(x)\mathcal{D}_{k-1}(x,t)\right],\ \mbox{for}\ k\geq 1\,,\end{cases}

(10.14)

where, by induction, we have defined, for
k\geq 0

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}_{0}(x,t):=D\mathfrak{c}_{0}(x)+\mathfrak{c}_{0}(x)\log t\,Dp_{0}(x)\\
\mathcal{D}_{k+1}(x,t):=D\mathfrak{c}_{k+1}(x)+\mathfrak{c}_{k+1}(x)\mathfrak{c}_{k}(x)[{\bf e}_{k-1}(x,t)]^{p_{k}(x)}Dp_{k+1}(x)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathfrak{c}_{k+1}(x)p_{k+1}(x)[{\bf e}_{k-1}(x,t)]^{p_{k}(x)}\mathcal{D}_{k}(x,t)\,.\end{cases}

(10.15)

Using (
10.14
)-(
10.15
), for
k\geq 1
we compute, again by induction

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ \partial_{x}\Pi_{0}(x,t)=0\,,\quad\mathcal{L}_{0}(x,t)=0_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\\
\ \partial_{x}\Pi_{k}(x,t)=\Pi_{k}(x,t)\mathcal{L}_{k}(x,t)\\
\ \mathcal{L}_{k}(x,t):=D\log\left(\mathfrak{c}_{k-1}(x)p_{k-1}(x)\right)\\
\ \quad+[{\bf e}_{k-2}(x,t)]^{p_{k-1}(x)}\left[\mathfrak{c}_{k-1}(x)Dp_{k}(x)+p_{k}(x)\mathcal{D}_{k-1}(x,t)\right]+\mathcal{L}_{k-1}(x,t)\,.\end{cases}

(10.16)

Finally, using (
10.14
)-(
10.16
) in (
10.12
), for every
k\geq 0
we conclude with

\displaystyle\partial_{x}{\bf e}^{\prime}_{k}(x,t)={\bf e}^{\prime}_{k}(x,t)\left[D\left(\log(\mathfrak{c}_{k}(x)p_{k}(x))\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 48.36967pt+\log tDp_{0}(x)+\mathcal{L}_{k}(x,t)+[{\bf e}_{k-1}(x,t)]^{p_{k}(x)}\mathcal{D}_{k}(x,t)\right].

(10.17)

As for the tensor
\partial_{zz}{\bf e}_{k}(x,|z|)
, by a direct computation we have that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{zz}{\bf e}_{k}(x,|z|)\xi\cdot\xi\geq\min\left\{{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime\prime}(x,|z|),\frac{{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,|z|)}{|z|}\right\}|\xi|^{2}\\
|\partial_{zz}{\bf e}_{k}(x,|z|)|^{2}=[{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime\prime}(x,|z|)]^{2}+\left[\frac{{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,|z|)}{|z|}\right]^{2}(Nn-1)\end{cases}

(10.18)

hold for every choice of
z,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
with
z\not=0
and
x\in\Omega
.

Step 2: Determining
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
and
g_{3}
.

For every fixed
k\geq 0
, the constants implied in the symbols
\lesssim
and
\approx
, will depend on
\texttt{data}_{k}
and we shall use the auxiliary functions

\mathfrak{h}_{k}(x):=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\,[|D\mathfrak{c}_{i}(x)|+|Dp_{i}(x)|]+1\,,\qquad k\geq 0\,.

It follows that
\mathfrak{h}_{k}\in L^{d}(\Omega)
for
d>n
, by assumptions. By (
10.15
) it follows
|\mathcal{D}_{0}(x,t)|\lesssim\mathfrak{h}_{0}(x)[|\log t|+1]
, so that induction gives that

|\mathcal{D}_{k}(x,t)|\lesssim\mathfrak{h}_{k}(x)\left[(t^{p_{0}(x)}|\log t|+1)\Pi_{k-1}(x,t)+1\right]\quad\mbox{holds for all $k\geq 1$}\,.

In turn, this and (
10.16
) imply that

|\mathcal{L}_{k}(x,t)|\lesssim\mathfrak{h}_{k}(x)\left[(t^{p_{0}(x)}|\log t|+1)\Pi_{k-1}(x,t)+1\right]\quad\mbox{holds for all $k\geq 1$}\,.

By (
10.12
) it is also
{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)\lesssim t^{p_{0}(x)-1}\leq t^{p_{\mathfrak{m}}-1}
for
t\approx 0
. From this and (
10.3
) it follows there exit constants
m_{k}\equiv m_{k}(\texttt{data}_{k})\geq 1
such that, for a.e.
x\in\Omega

\begin{cases}\lvert\partial_{x}{\bf e}^{\prime}_{k}(x,t)\rvert\lesssim m_{k}\mathfrak{h}_{k}(x)&\ \mbox{for all}\ \ t\in[0,{\rm e}]\\
\lvert\partial_{x}{\bf e}^{\prime}_{k}(x,t)\rvert\lesssim\mathfrak{h}_{k}(x){\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)t^{p_{0}(x)}\log t\Pi_{k}(x,t)&\ \mbox{for all}\ \ t\in[{\rm e},\infty)\,.\end{cases}

(10.19)

Now, let
\{c_{k},d_{k}\}
be constants larger than
1
, to be eventually chosen large enough, again in dependence on
\texttt{data}_{k}
, and
\phi\in C([0,\infty),[0,1])
be a non-decreasing function such that
\phi(t)=0
for
t\in[0,{\rm e}/2]
and
\phi(t)=1
for
t\in[{\rm e},\infty)
. With
p_{\mathfrak{m}}
as in (
1.20
), and
x\in\Omega
and
t>0
, we define

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ g_{1}(x,t)\equiv g_{1,k}(x,t):=\phi(t)\min\{p_{\mathfrak{m}}-1,1\}{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)/t\\
\ g_{2}(x,t)\equiv g_{2,k}(x,t):=\phi(t)c_{k}t^{p_{0}(x)}\Pi_{k}(x,t){\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)/t\\
\ g_{3}(x,t)\equiv g_{3,k}(x,t):=(1-\phi(t))c_{k}m_{k}\\
\hskip 108.12047pt+\phi(t)c_{k}m_{k}{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)t^{p_{0}(x)}\log t\Pi_{k}(x,t)\,.\end{cases}

We are ready to check that
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
satisfy the relations prescribed in Section
4.1
and required to meet the assumptions of Theorem
4.1
. We take
c_{\textnormal{b}}\equiv c_{\textnormal{b}}(\texttt{data}_{k})\geq 1
to be determined in due course of the proof,
T={\rm e},\nu\approx\min\{p_{\mathfrak{m}}-1,1\},\gamma=p_{\mathfrak{m}},\mu=0
; moreover, any choice of small numbers
\sigma,\hat{\sigma}>0
will work. In this way it is
\bar{G}(x,t)=\min\{p_{\mathfrak{m}}-1,1\}[{\bf e}_{k}(x,t)-{\bf e}_{k}(x,{\rm e})+({\rm e}^{2}+1)^{p_{\mathfrak{m}}/2}]
for
t\geq{\rm e}
, so that
\bar{G}(x,t)\approx{\bf e}_{k}(x,t)
for
t
large. By (
10.12
)-(
10.3
) and (
10.18
), it follows that (
4.2
)
2,3
are satisfied provided we take
c_{k}\equiv c_{k}(\texttt{data}_{k})
large enough. Similarly, (
4.2
)
4
follows using (
10.19
) and eventually increasing
c_{k}
. In the same way (
4.4
) holds with the above choice of the parameters by (
10.12
). As for (
4.16
)-(
4.18
), note that, given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists
c\equiv c(\texttt{data}_{k},\varepsilon)
, such that
\Pi_{k}(x,t)t^{p_{0}(x)+1}\log t\leq c[{\bf e}_{k}(x,t)]^{\varepsilon}
for
t\geq{\rm e}
. Using this, (
4.16
) follows, for all
\hat{\sigma}>0
, from (
10.19
) by taking
h\equiv d_{k}\mathfrak{h}_{k}\in L^{d}(\Omega)
, provided we take
d_{k}\equiv d_{k}(\texttt{data}_{k},\hat{\sigma})
large enough. As for (
4.17
), note that
(g_{3}^{2}/g_{1})(x,t)\lesssim[{\bf e}_{k}^{\prime}(x,t)]^{1+\varepsilon}\lesssim[{\bf e}_{k}(x,t)]^{1+2\varepsilon}
for every
\varepsilon>0
and
t\geq{\rm e}
; therefore (
4.17
) follows for every
\sigma\in(0,1)
, again taking
c_{\textnormal{b}}\equiv c_{\textnormal{b}}(\texttt{data}_{k},\sigma)
large enough. In the same way (
4.18
) follows, by eventually enlarging
c_{\textnormal{b}}
. This means that the assumptions of Theorem
4.1
are satisfied and the proof of Theorem
1.5
is complete.

10.4
Proof of Theorem
1.6

Conditions in (
4.1
) are verified by (
1.4
); indeed, an easy consequence of (
1.4
) is that
t\mapsto\tilde{a}(t)/t^{i_{\textnormal{a}}}
is non-decreasing. It follows that
\tilde{a}(t)t\leq\tilde{a}(1)t^{i_{\textnormal{a}}+1}
for
t\in(0,1]
, and therefore
t\mapsto A(x,t)\in C^{1}_{{\rm loc}}[0,\infty)\cap C^{2}_{{\rm loc}}(0,\infty)
for every
x\in\Omega
as it is
i_{\textnormal{a}}>-1
. Moreover

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ \lvert\partial_{zz}A(x,\lvert z\rvert)\rvert\leq L\sqrt{Nn}\max\{1,s_{\textnormal{a}}+1\}\tilde{a}(\lvert z\rvert)\\
\ \nu\min\{1,i_{\textnormal{a}}+1\}\tilde{a}(\lvert z\rvert)\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}\leq\partial_{zz}A(x,\lvert z\rvert)\xi\cdot\xi\\
\ \lvert\partial_{xz}A(x,\lvert z\rvert)\rvert\leq\lvert D\mathfrak{c}(x)\rvert\tilde{a}(\lvert z\rvert)\lvert z\rvert\,,\end{cases}

hold for every choice of
x\in\Omega
and
z,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
,
|z|\not=0
. Notice that here we again indicate
by
\partial_{zz}A(\cdot)
the Hessian of
z\to A(\cdot,|z|)
. Having in mind to apply Theorem
4.2
, this leads to define
g_{1}(t):=\nu\min\{1,i_{\textnormal{a}}+1\}\tilde{a}(t),g_{2}(t):=LNn\max\{1,s_{\textnormal{a}}+1\}\tilde{a}(t),g_{3}(t):=\tilde{a}(t)t
and
h:=\lvert D\mathfrak{c}\rvert\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
. As
t\mapsto\tilde{a}(t)/t^{i_{\textnormal{a}}}
is non-decreasing, (
4.4
) is also verified with
\gamma=i_{\textnormal{a}}+2
,
\mu=0
. The definitions given above make sure that conditions
(
4.19
)
are satisfied with

c_{\textnormal{u}}\approx\max\{1,s_{\textnormal{a}}+1\}/\min\{1,i_{\textnormal{a}}+1\}
.
This means that Theorem
4.2
applies and (
1.24
) follows from (
4.23
).

11
Obstacle problems and Theorem
1.8

11.1
A general result

We start with the following constrained analog of Theorem
4.1
, which will be used to ge the proof of Theorem
1.8
:

Theorem 11.1

Let
u\in W^{1,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
be a constrained minimizer of the functional
\mathcal{F}_{0}
in (
1.25
), under assumptions
set
in
(
4.20
)
with
g_{3},\partial_{zz}F
being locally bounded on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)
and
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
, respectively, and
\gamma\geq 2
. If
\psi\in W^{2,1}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega)
with
\lvert D^{2}\psi\rvert\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
, then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
.

Proof

In
set
we have initially assumed that
g_{3}
was locally bounded in
\Omega\times(0,\infty)
, while here we are assuming it is locally bounded in
\Omega\times[0,\infty)
, that means that for every
\Omega_{0}\Subset\Omega
and
b\geq 0
we have that
\sup_{\Omega_{0}\times[0,b]}\,g_{3}
is finite. This is is not really an additional assumption as it is automatically satisfied in all the cases considered for instance in Theorem
1.8
.
Notice also that
D^{2}\psi\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)\subset L(n,1)
implies that
D\psi
is continuous in
\Omega

St
and, in particular,
D\psi\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any ball
\mathcal{B}\Subset\Omega
; accordingly, we let
T_{\psi}\equiv T_{\psi}(\mathcal{B}):=\|D\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B})}+T+1
. We now fix an arbitrary ball
\mathcal{B}\Subset\Omega
with
\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})\leq 1
, we consider the family
\{F_{\varepsilon}\}
constructed in Section
5.1
. This time we take
0<\varepsilon<\min\{1/T_{\psi},T\}/4
and define
u_{\varepsilon}
as the solution to the auxiliary problem

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}\mapsto\min_{(u+W^{1,\gamma}_{0}(\mathcal{B}))\cap\mathcal{K}_{\psi}(\mathcal{B})}\int_{\mathcal{B}}F_{\varepsilon}(x,Dw)\,dx\,.

(11.1)

The existence of
u_{\varepsilon}
follows by standard theory, and the variational inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}}\partial_{z}F_{\varepsilon}(x,Du_{\varepsilon})\cdot(Dw-Du_{\varepsilon})\,dx\geq 0

(11.2)

holds for all
w\in(u+W^{1,\gamma}_{0}(\mathcal{B}))\cap\mathcal{K}_{\psi}(\mathcal{B})
.
Thanks to the
\gamma
-polynomial growth conditions in (
5.14
), we are now able to perform the linearization procedure used in
(
fumin
, page 237)
i.e., we can rearrange (
11.2
) in the following way:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}}\left[\partial_{z}F_{\varepsilon}(x,Du_{\varepsilon})\cdot D\varphi-\texttt{f}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\varphi\right]\,dx=0

(11.3)

for all
\varphi\in W^{1,\gamma}_{0}(\mathcal{B})
, where

\texttt{f}_{\varepsilon}(x):=-\theta_{\varepsilon}(x)\mathds{1}_{\{x\in\mathcal{B}\colon u_{\varepsilon}(x)=\psi(x)\}}\,{\rm div}\,\partial_{z}F_{\varepsilon}(x,D\psi)\,,

for some measurable density
\theta_{\varepsilon}\colon\mathcal{B}\to[0,1]
. Note that the definition of
\texttt{f}_{\varepsilon}
makes sense in light of the fact that
\psi\in W^{2,n}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
and of the discussion made at the beginning of Section
6
to prove (
6.8
). This implies that
\partial_{z}F_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,D\psi)\in W^{1,n}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the usual chain rule formula holds as in Section
6
, again thanks to the results in
dele
; see Remark
10
below. We then define the constant

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{\psi}(\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle:=\|g_{3}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}\times[0,T_{\psi}))}+\|\tilde{a}(\cdot,1)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B},|z|\in[0,T_{\psi})}|\partial_{zz}F(x,z)|+T_{\psi}^{\gamma-2}+1\,.

(11.4)

This quantity is always bounded as
\partial_{zz}F
is in turn assumed to be locally bounded and
\gamma\geq 2
, here we also use the fact that
g_{3}
is locally bounded as again described in the statement of the Theorem. This is essentially the only place where such assumptions come into the play.
Elementary manipulations based on the first property in (
4.4
) and (
5.13
)
3
, give

\lvert\texttt{f}_{\varepsilon}(x)\rvert\leq c(n,N,\nu,\gamma,c_{\textnormal{b}})\mathfrak{g}_{\psi}(\mathcal{B})[\lvert D^{2}\psi(x)\rvert+h(x)]\,.

(11.5)

Here we have also used that
\varepsilon\leq 1/T_{\psi}
to exploit the definitions in (
5.3
) and (
5.9
).
In turn, (
11.5
) gives

\displaystyle\texttt{f}_{\varepsilon}\in\mathfrak{X}(B)\quad\mbox{with}\ \ \lVert\texttt{f}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(B)}\leq c\lVert D^{2}\psi\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(B)}+c\lVert h\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(B)}\,,

(11.6)

for all balls
B\subseteq\mathcal{B}
, where
c\equiv c(n,N,\nu,\gamma,c_{\textnormal{b}},g_{\psi}(B))
. Since
u_{\varepsilon}
verifies (

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}\mapsto\min_{u+W^{1,\gamma}_{0}(\mathcal{B})}\int_{\mathcal{B}}\left[F_{\varepsilon}(x,Dw)-\texttt{f}_{\varepsilon}\cdot w\right]\,dx\,.

(11.7)

By (

in Section

below, therefore

in view of an application to solutions to (

),
becomes

\displaystyle\|F_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,Du_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}+\|Du_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{B})}^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c\|F(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}+c\lVert D^{2}\psi\rVert_{L^{n}(\mathcal{B})}^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)}+c\lVert h\rVert_{L^{n}(\mathcal{B})}^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)}+c

thanks to (

when

when

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt\leq\frac{c}{(1-s)^{\beta}[\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{\beta}}\left[\|F(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}^{\theta}+\lVert D^{2}\psi\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B})}^{\theta}+\lVert h\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B})}^{\theta}+1\right]\,,

Remark 10

The linearization procedure leading to (
11.7
) has been first introduced in
fuobs
, and it is usually employed assuming that
\psi\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathcal{B})

fumin
. A careful examination of the proof reveals that
\psi\in W^{2,n}(\mathcal{B})\cap W^{1,\infty}(\mathcal{B})
suffices, that in turn implies
\partial_{z}F_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,D\psi)\in W^{1,n}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For this, a first basic remark is that the weak integration-by-parts formula
\int_{\mathcal{B}}h\varphi_{x_{i}}\,dx=-\int_{\mathcal{B}}h_{x_{i}}\varphi\,dx
holds for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, whenever
h\in W^{1,n}(\mathcal{B})
and
\varphi\in W^{1,\gamma}_{0}(\mathcal{B})
, for some
\gamma>1
. In turn, this follows by a standard density argument and Sobolev embedding in the limiting case.

11.2
Proof of Theorem
1.8
and additional results

The proof of Theorem
11.1
offers a route to get the obstacle version of all the other results presented in the unconstrained case; in particular, Theorem
1.8
follows. The key point is again to employ
the linearization procedure used in
fuobs
;
fumin
to pass from a variational inequality as in (
11.2
) to an equation as in (
11.3
), to which the estimates in the unconstrained case immediately apply. The whole procedure then works provided the additional assumptions
\gamma\geq 2
and
g_{3},\partial_{zz}F\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}
are in force as described in the statement of Theorem
11.1
. With this path being settled, the reader can now easily obtain the constrained extensions of all the results presented in this paper. A few remarks are in order. First, notice that Theorems
1.1
-
1.2
and Theorems
1.4
-
1.5
in the constrained version, are a direct consequence of Theorem
11.1
and this can be checked exactly as in the unconstrained version. Next, again as for the case of Theorem
11.1
, Theorems
4.2
and
4.4
admit a constrained reformulation. In turn, the former would imply a constrained version of Theorem
1.6
. The latter would instead imply a first constrained version of Theorem
1.3
, where the bounds in (
1.19
) appear in the
<
-version; see also Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
1.3
. As for the full
\leq
-version in (
1.19
), it is then necessary to readapt to the obstacle case the arguments from Step 2 and 3 of the proof of Theorem
1.3
in Section
10.2
, along the lines of the proof of Theorem
11.1
. In this respect, the only worth mentioning difference is that the higher integrability lemmas
12
and
13
can be easily obtained in the setting of obstacle problems too, starting from the arguments indicated here. For this see also
chde
. Finally, note that in the case of functionals with
(p,q)
-growth, including the double phase one in (
1.10
), verifying the assumptions
\gamma\geq 2
and
\partial_{zz}F\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}
boils down to assume that
p\geq 2
, as indeed done in Theorem
1.8
. The additional (micro)assumption on
g_{3}
in the statement of Theorem
11.1
is instead satisfied in every case.

Remark 11

In Theorem
11.1
we can trade the assumption
\gamma\geq 2
with
\mu>0
(non-degenerate case). This eventually leads to constrained versions of Theorems
1.1
-
1.2
assuming that
p>1
instead of
p\geq 2
, provided it is
\mu>0
.

12
Justification of a priori regularity

In this final section we justify the claim in (
6.5
), which is necessary to carry out the rest of the estimates in Sections
6
and
8
. Keeping (
5.6
) in mind, we therefore consider a functional like (
1.1
), with the integrand
F
being convex in the gradient variable, satisfying the structure condition (
4.1
)
1
, where we assume that
\tilde{F}
and
\tilde{F}^{\prime}
are continuous functions on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)
such that
\tilde{F}(x,0)\equiv 0
, that
\partial_{zz}F,\partial_{xz}F
are measurable and

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ t\mapsto\tilde{F}(x,t)\in C^{1}_{\textnormal{loc}}[0,\infty)&\mbox{for all}\ \ x\in\Omega\\
\ t\mapsto\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)=:\tilde{a}(x,t)t\in\textnormal{Lip}_{{\rm loc}}[0,\infty)&\mbox{for all}\ \ x\in\Omega\\
\ x\mapsto\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)\in W(1;\mathfrak{X})(\Omega)\quad&\mbox{for every}\ \ t\geq 0\,.\end{cases}

(12.1)

The last condition means that
|\partial_{x}\tilde{F}^{\prime}(\cdot,t)|\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
for every
t\geq 0
, where
\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)
has been defined in (
1.9
). In particular, this implies that
x\mapsto\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)\in W^{1,n}(\Omega)
. As in Section
6
, we also assume that the set of non-differentiability points of
t\mapsto\tilde{a}(x,t)t
is independent of
x
and that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ \nu_{0}(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\gamma/2}-\Lambda\mu^{\gamma}\leq F(x,z)\leq\Lambda(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2})^{\gamma/2}\\
\ \lvert\partial_{zz}F(x,z)\rvert\leq\Lambda(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\\
\ \nu_{0}(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}\leq\partial_{zz}F(x,z)\xi\cdot\xi\\
\ \lvert\partial_{xz}F(x,z)\rvert\leq\Lambda h(x)(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(\gamma-1)/2}\\
\ f,h\in\mathfrak{X}(\Omega)\end{cases}

(12.2)

hold for every
x\in\Omega
, and for every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
, provided
\partial_{zz}F(x,z)
exists, and a.e.
x
and
z,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
in the case of (
12.2
)
4
. In
(
12.2
)
, it is
\gamma>1
,
0<\nu_{0}\leq 1\leq\Lambda
,
0<\mu\leq 1
. This functional is of the type considered in (
6.2
) by (
5.14
) and (
5.23
), so that the claim in (
6.5
) is justified by the following:

Proposition 6

Let
u\in W^{1,\gamma}_{{\rm loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a minimizer of
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1
), under assumptions
(
12.1
)
-
(
12.2
)
.
Then
Du\in L^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
and
u\in W^{2,2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
.

Proof

The proof goes now in three different steps, where we essentially revisit and readapt a few hidden facts in the literature. References
DeMi
and
KM
are particularly relevant here.

Step 1: Regularized integrands.

We revisit the procedure we used in
(
DeMi
, Theorem 4, Step 1)
and start fixing a ball
\mathcal{B}\Subset\Omega
such that
\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})\leq 1
. We first extend
\tilde{F}
by even reflection making it defined on
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,
\tilde{F}(x,t):=\tilde{F}(x,-t)
, and then we consider standard, radially symmetric mollifiers
\phi_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{\rm{c}}(B_{1}),\phi_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{\rm{c}}(-1,1),\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\phi_{2}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=1,\phi_{1,\delta}(x):=\phi_{1}(x/\delta)/\delta^{n}
,
\phi_{2,\delta}(t):=\phi(t/\delta)/\delta
,
B_{3/4}(0)\subset\textnormal{supp}\,\phi_{1},(-3/4,3/4)\subset\textnormal{supp}\,\phi_{2}
. With
\delta
such that
0<\delta<\left(0,\,{\rm dist}(\mathcal{B},\partial\Omega)/2\right)
, we define

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{\delta}(x,t):=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{B_{1}}\tilde{F}(x+\delta y,t+\delta s)\phi_{1}(y)\phi_{2}(s)\,dy\,ds\\
h_{\delta}(x):=(h*\phi_{1,\delta})(x)\end{cases}

(12.3)

for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 15

If
F_{\delta}(x,z):=\tilde{F}_{\delta}(x,\langle z\rangle)
, where
\langle z\rangle:=\sqrt{|z|^{2}+\delta^{2}}
, then

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ \frac{1}{c_{\textnormal{r}}}(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{\gamma/2}-c_{\textnormal{r}}\mu_{\delta}^{\gamma}\leq F_{\delta}(x,z)\leq c_{\textnormal{r}}(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{\gamma/2}\\
\ \lvert\partial_{zz}F_{\delta}(x,z)\rvert\leq c_{\textnormal{r}}(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\\
\ \frac{1}{c_{\textnormal{r}}}(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}\leq\partial_{zz}F_{\delta}(x,z)\xi\cdot\xi\\
\ \lvert\partial_{xz}F_{\delta}(x,z)\rvert\leq c_{\textnormal{r}}h_{\delta}(x)(\lvert z\rvert^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-1)/2}\end{cases}

(12.4)

hold for every
x\in\Omega^{\prime}
,
z,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
, where
c_{\textnormal{r}}\equiv c_{\textnormal{r}}(n,N,\nu_{0},\Lambda,\gamma)\geq 1
is a positive constant, and, as usual, it is
\mu_{\delta}:=\mu+\delta
. Moreover, we have

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}F_{\delta}(x,z)\to F(x,z)\ \ \mbox{uniformly on compact subsets of $\overline{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$ as $\delta\to 0$}\\[3.0pt]
\lVert h_{\delta}\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(B)}\leq c\lVert h\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(B+\delta B_{1}(0))}\end{array}\right.

(12.5)

whenever
B\subset\mathcal{B}
is a ball such that
B+\delta B_{1}(0)\Subset\Omega
, where
c
is independent of
\delta
and
h(\cdot)
.

Proof

The arguments we are going to use here build on those employed in
(
DeMi
, Section 4.5)
. The upper bound in (
12.4
)
1
follows directly from the definition (
12.3
), while the lower bound follows verbatim from
(
DeMi
, Section 4.5, last display)
, upon replacing
\gamma-2
there with
\gamma
here, and taking the case
n=1
there. We now go for (
12.4
)
3
. Denoting
\tilde{a}_{\delta}(x,t):=\tilde{F}_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,t)/t
for
t\not=0
, we note that (
12.4
)
3
follows from

\begin{cases}(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\lesssim\tilde{a}_{\delta}(x,t)\\
(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\lesssim\tilde{a}_{\delta}(x,t)+\tilde{a}_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,t)t=\tilde{F}_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}(x,t)\end{cases}

(12.6)

for
t\geq\delta
.
This in turn follows observing that

\partial_{zz}F_{\delta}(x,z)=a_{\delta}(x,\langle z\rangle)\mathds{I}_{N\times n}+a_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,\langle z\rangle)\langle z\rangle\frac{z\otimes z}{\langle z\rangle^{2}}\,,

(12.7)

holds for every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
, and recalling the arguments for (
5.15
)-(
5.16
) and Lemma
7
; also observe that
\langle z\rangle^{2}+\mu_{\delta}^{2}\approx|z|^{2}+\mu_{\delta}^{2}
. Here, as in the rest of the proof of the Lemma, all the implied constants in the symbol
\lesssim
depend only on
n,N,\nu_{0},\Lambda
and
\gamma
, but remain otherwise independent of
\delta
.
To prove (
12.6
)
1
, note that
\tilde{F}_{\delta}(x,t)
is still such that
\tilde{F}_{\delta}(x,t)=\tilde{F}_{\delta}(x,-t)
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
so that
\tilde{F}_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,0)=0
. Moreover, from (
12.2
)
3
, arguing as in (
4.10
), it follows that
(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\approx\tilde{F}^{\prime\prime}(x,t)
, for
t\not=0
, provided
F^{\prime\prime}(x,t)
exists. From this and the definition in (
12.3
), following again the same argument in
(
DeMi
, Section 4.5, last two displays)
, we gain
(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\lesssim\tilde{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\delta}(x,t)
, which is (
12.6
)
2
. In turn, integrating this last inequality and using
\tilde{F}_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,0)=0
, yields
(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}t/\max\{1,\gamma-1\}\leq\int_{0}^{t}(s^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\,ds\lesssim\tilde{F}^{\prime}_{\delta}(x,t)
for
t>0
, which is in fact (
12.6
)
1
and (
12.4
)
3
is verified. We now derive (
12.4
)
2
. By (
12.7
), it is sufficient to prove that
\tilde{a}_{\delta}(x,t)+|\tilde{a}_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,t)t|\lesssim(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}
. For this we can again use the argument in
(
DeMi
, Proof of (4.52)
3
)
to start showing that
|\tilde{a}_{\delta}(x,t)+\tilde{a}_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,t)t|=|\tilde{F}_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}(x,t)|\lesssim(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}
for
t\geq\delta
. Therefore it remains to prove that
\tilde{a}_{\delta}(x,t)t=\tilde{F}_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,t)\lesssim(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}t
. Again by
\tilde{F}_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,0)=0
, it follows that

\tilde{F}_{\delta}^{\prime}(x,t)\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}(s^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}\,ds\leq\max\{1,1/(\gamma-1)\}(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}t
, and this completes the proof of (
12.4
)
2
.
For the proof of (
12.4
)
4
, note that this implies that
|\partial_{x}\tilde{F}^{\prime}(x,t)|\leq\Lambda h(x)(t^{2}+\mu^{2})^{(\gamma-1)/2}
, from which (
12.4
)
4
follows from the definitions in (
12.3
) as for (
12.4
)
1
. Finally, (
12.5
)
1
is again an immediate consequence of the definitions in (
12.3
) since
\tilde{F}
is continuous; (
12.5
)
2
follows from the definition of Lorentz and
L^{2}(\log L)^{\mathfrak{a}}
-norms and the boundedness of maximal operators in the corresponding spaces.

We further define
f_{\delta}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})
as
f_{\delta}(x):=f(x)
if
\lvert f(x)\rvert\leq 1/\delta
, and
f_{\delta}(x):=\delta^{-1}|f(x)|^{-1}f(x)
otherwise. Finally, we let
u_{\delta}\in u+W^{1,\gamma}_{0}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})
, defined as the unique solution of the Dirichlet problem

u_{\delta}\mapsto\min_{w\in u+W^{1,\gamma}_{0}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{N})}\int_{\mathcal{B}}\left[F_{\delta}(x,Dw)-f_{\delta}\cdot w\right]\,dx\,.

Up to now, we have required that
\delta
is small enough to have
\delta<\,{\rm dist}(\mathcal{B},\partial\Omega)/2
. In the next step we shall choose additional smallness conditions on
\delta
.

Step 2:
Du\in L^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})

Thanks to (
12.4
), standard regularity theory yields

\begin{cases}Du_{\delta}\in L^{\infty}_{{\rm loc}}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}),\quad u_{\delta}\in W^{2,2}_{{\rm loc}}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{N})\\
\partial_{z}F_{\delta}(x,Du_{\delta})\in W^{1,2}_{{\rm loc}}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{N\times n})\,.\end{cases}

(12.8)

See, for instance,
tolk
.
We can therefore proceed exactly as in the proof of Proposition
5
arguing on the Euler-Lagrange
-\,{\rm div}\,\partial_{z}F_{\delta}(x,Du_{\delta})=f_{\delta}
. This yields the existence of
\delta_{0}\equiv\delta_{0}(n,N,\nu_{0},\gamma,\Lambda,h(\cdot))\in(0,1)
and
R_{*}\equiv R_{*}(n,N,\nu_{0},\gamma,\linebreak\Lambda,h(\cdot))\leq 1
such that the estimate

\displaystyle\|Du_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(s\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c}{[(1-s)\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{n/\gamma}}\left[\|F_{\delta}(\cdot,Du_{\delta})\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}^{1/\gamma}+1\right]+c\|f_{\delta}\|_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B})}^{1/(\gamma-1)}

(12.9)

holds whenever
s\in(0,1)
, provided
\delta\leq\min\{\delta_{0},\,{\rm dist}(\mathcal{B},\partial\Omega)/2\}
and
\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})\leq R_{*}
, where
c\equiv c(n,N,\nu_{0},\Lambda,\gamma)\geq 1
is independent of
\delta
.
Indeed, the setting of Proposition
5
applies with the obvious choices
g_{1,\varepsilon}(t)\equiv(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}/c_{\textnormal{r}}
,
g_{2,\varepsilon}(t)\equiv c_{\textnormal{r}}(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}
,
g_{3,\varepsilon}(t)\equiv c_{\textnormal{r}}(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}t
,
h(\cdot)\equiv h_{\delta}(\cdot)
and
T\equiv\mu_{\delta}
; note that

(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-1)/2}\leq\sqrt{2}(t^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\delta})^{(\gamma-2)/2}t
for
t\geq\mu_{\delta}
.
Moreover, the only qualitative properties of the solution
u_{\delta}
needed to argue as in Proposition
5
are those in (
6.5
), that are exactly those in (
12.8
). Therefore the whole set of estimates developed there applies here verbatim once we observe that (
5.13
)
3
in only needed here when
t\geq T
, which is the case thanks to (
12.4
)
4
. Notice that, proceeding as in Proposition
5
, and recalling (
8.10
) (here applied with
h\equiv h_{\delta}
), the radius
R_{*}
here should exhibit a dependence on
h_{\delta}
, and therefore ultimately on
\delta
. However,
R_{*}
can be made independent of
\delta
, thanks to (
12.5
)
2
by further taking
\delta
small enough, and without creating vicious circles. Specifically, we arrive at (
Proof
) with the above choice of
g_{1,\varepsilon},g_{2,\varepsilon},g_{3,\varepsilon}
and (
8.10
) turns out to be

c_{*}\lVert h_{\delta}\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B})}\leq 1/6
, where
c_{*}
is independent of
\delta
but only depends of
\texttt{data}_{\textnormal{u}}
. With the current choice of the parameters this means that
c_{*}
depends only on
n,N,\nu_{0},\Lambda,\gamma,\mathfrak{a}
. We use (
12.5
)
2
to reduce the last condition to

cc_{*}\lVert h\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B}+\delta B_{1}(0))}\leq 1/6
, where
c
is the constant appearing in (
12.5
)
2
and it is independent of
\delta
. Therefore, by absolute continuity we find
\delta_{0},R_{*}\equiv\delta_{0},R_{*}(n,N,\nu_{0},\gamma,\Lambda,h(\cdot))
as described above, such that the last inequality is satisfied. This allows to set inequality (
12.9
) free from any dependence on
\delta
.
Next, using (
12.2
)
1
and (
12.4
)
1
, and finally the minimality of
u_{\delta}
in (
12.9
), we gain

\displaystyle\|Du_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(s\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c}{[(1-s)\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{n/\gamma}}\left[\|F(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}^{1/\gamma}+1\right]+c\|f\|_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B})}^{1/(\gamma-1)}

(12.10)

with
c
being independent of
\delta
.
From this, a standard convergence argument based on (
12.5
)
1
(see again the proof of Theorem
4.1
) extracting a subsequence
\{u_{\delta}\equiv u_{\delta_{j}}\}
such that
u_{\delta}\rightharpoonup^{*}u
weakly in
W^{1,\infty}(s\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{N})
, leads to

\|Du\|_{L^{\infty}(s\mathcal{B})}\leq\frac{c}{[(1-s)\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{n/\gamma}}\left[\|F(\cdot,Du)\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B})}^{1/\gamma}+1\right]+c\|f\|_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{B})}^{1/(\gamma-1)}\,.

As this holds whenever
\mathcal{B}\Subset\Omega
, then
Du
is locally bounded.

Step 3:
u\in W^{2,2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N})

For this we shall revisit some arguments from
(
KM
, Theorems 4.5-4.6)
. We test the weak formulation of the Euler-Lagrange system
-\,{\rm div}\,\partial_{z}F_{\delta}(x,Du_{\delta})=f_{\delta}
by
D_{s}\varphi
, for
s\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{B})
; integration by parts yields

\int_{\mathcal{B}}D_{s}[\partial F_{\delta}(x,Du_{\delta})]\cdot D\varphi\,dx=-\int_{\mathcal{B}}f_{\delta}\cdot D_{s}\varphi\,dx\,.

(12.11)

We then take
\eta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}/2;[0,1])
with
\eta\equiv 1
in
\mathcal{B}/4
,
\|D\eta\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B})}\lesssim 1/[\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]
, and we define
\varphi\equiv\varphi_{s}:=\eta^{2}D_{s}u_{\delta}
so that
\varphi\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\mathcal{B};\mathbb{R}^{N})
and has compact support in
\mathcal{B}
. Using
\varphi
as test function in (
12.11
), summing over
s\in\{1,\cdots n\}
and taking (
12.4
) into account, yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}}(|Du_{\delta}|^{2}+\mu_{\delta}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}|D^{2}u_{\delta}|^{2}\eta^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\quad\leq c\int_{\mathcal{B}}\eta(|Du_{\delta}|^{2}+\mu_{\delta}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}|D^{2}u_{\delta}||Du_{\delta}||D\eta|\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+c\int_{\mathcal{B}}h_{\delta}(|Du_{\delta}|^{2}+\mu_{\delta}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-1}{2}}[\eta^{2}|D^{2}u_{\delta}|+\eta|D\eta||Du_{\delta}|]\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{s=1}^{n}\int_{B}|f_{\delta}||D\varphi_{s}|\,dx\,.

Estimating the third integral in a standard way (see for instance in
(
KM
, pag. 395)
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}}(|Du_{\delta}|^{2}+\mu_{\delta}^{2})^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}|D^{2}u_{\delta}|^{2}\eta^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c[\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{-2}\int_{\mathcal{B}}\eta[1+h_{\delta}^{2}](|Du_{\delta}|^{2}+\mu_{\delta}^{2})^{\gamma/2}\,dx

(12.12)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+c[\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B})]^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}\|Du_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B}/2)}+c\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}\|\eta^{2}D^{2}u_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}\,.

The involved constant
c
only depends on
n,N,\nu,\Lambda
and
\gamma
and is otherwise independent of
\delta\in(0,1)
.
We now set

M:=\sup_{\delta}\,\|Du_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}/2)}+1,

which is a finite quantity by (
12.10
). We start considering the case
\gamma\geq 2
, where we have

\displaystyle\mu^{\gamma-2}\|\eta D^{2}u_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\|1+h_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}^{2}(M^{2}+\mu_{\delta}^{2})^{\gamma/2}+c\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}M

\displaystyle\qquad+c\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}\|\eta^{2}D^{2}u_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}

where
c\equiv c(n,N,\nu_{0},\Lambda,\gamma,\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B}))

and therefore, via Young inequality, we get

\displaystyle\|D^{2}u_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B}/4)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\mu^{2-\gamma}\|1+h_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}^{2}[M^{2}+1]^{\gamma/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+c\mu^{2-\gamma}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}M+c\mu^{2(2-\gamma)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})}^{2}

which is a uniform (with respect to
\delta
) local bound for
\{D^{2}u_{\delta}\}
:

\|D^{2}u_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B}/4)}\leq c(n,N,\nu_{0},\gamma,\Lambda,\|h\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})},\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{B})},\texttt{r}(\mathcal{B}),M,\mu)\,.

(12.13)

In the case
1<\gamma<2
, we can argue exactly as after (
12.12
), but replacing
\mu
by
M
, thereby getting again (
12.13
). Starting from (
12.13
), using the same approximation argument for the proof of Theorem
4.1
and in Step 2 here, we can let
\delta\to 0
(via a subsequence) in (
12.13
) finally getting a local upper bound for
D^{2}u
in
L^{2}
. The assertion then follows via the usual covering argument.
